There was a knot of yellow light a quarter mile ahead where the road went into a long shallow dip — not station light, not the flat white lamp on its pole, but the moving orange of real flame, four or five of them, low down, and shapes going about in front of them. People. A cart or a barrow. Somebody with a lamp on a pole walking a line and stopping.

A quarter mile. Four hundred paces. At the pace he could make with the frame on, five minutes.

The nearest hound came up out of the ditch onto the shoulder.

It did it in one movement, without hurrying, and now it was on the road bed with him, twenty feet off his left elbow, going along at his speed, and in the amber light he could see the whole length of it and it was as long as he was tall.

Five minutes was too long.

---

He knew that the way he had known, standing on a gravel bench above a flooded creek, that the downstream detour was two and a half days he did not have. It was not a feeling. It was arithmetic, and he had been doing arithmetic on his feet for sixteen days.

He could not run. With the frame loaded he could make, at a shambling terrified trot, perhaps half again his walking speed, for about a hundred paces, and then he would go down. He had gone down on flat ground in daylight with nothing chasing him.

He could not fight like this. He had a blade on his right fist and a hundred and some pounds of brother strapped to his back and hips, and a rail that ran out past his own width on the right so that he could not turn quickly to that side without swinging his brother's broken leg through the air. He could not get out of the belt with one hand. He could not put his back against anything, because his back was where Tonk was.

He could not do both. That was the whole of it, and it arrived in him complete, in one piece, with no argument attached: *you cannot carry him and stand in front of him at the same time.*

So he looked for the corner.

---

The road went down into its dip, and the dip was a bad place, and even at a walk with five animals pacing him he could see it was a bad place.

The made surface broke up into slabs, tilted, with black water standing in the joints. The shoulders had slumped down into the ditches on both sides so that the road stopped having edges and started having slopes. And at the bottom of the dip something went under the road — he could hear it before he reached it, water running in a pipe — and where it went under, the old builders had put in stone.

There was a squared head of masonry at the lip of the culvert on the west side: a wing wall two courses higher than the shoulder, running back from the road edge into the bank at a right angle, with a flat coping on top at about the height of his chest. The angle it made with the road bed was a little hollow of dead grass about four feet across, floored with broken stone, walled on two sides.

Two sides. Not four. Two was what there was.

"Tonk," he said. "Listen to me and don't ask me anything yet."

"Is it the dogs?"

"Yes. I'm going to put you down."

"*No* —"

"Yes." He was already going to his knees beside the wall, and the whole of it had begun to happen very fast and very slowly at once. "You're going in the corner and I'm going to stand in front of you, and I can't do that with you on my back. That's the whole reason. There isn't another one."

He got the belt buckle. It took two hands and it took four seconds and he spent every one of those four seconds with his head up, looking over the coping, at the shapes standing in the road behind him doing nothing at all.

They were doing nothing at all. That was what they had been doing for ten minutes. They had had ten minutes to come at him on open road and they had not, and somewhere under the fear a small cold part of Jab's mind filed that and said: *they're not hunting you yet. They're counting.*

---

Getting a child with a broken leg out of a carrying frame took nine minutes if you did it properly.

He did it in about ninety seconds, and he did it wrong, and it was the worst thing he had ever done to his brother.

He had to take the ankle strap and the thigh strap and the chest band in an order that did not matter any more, and he had to take the leg off the rail without the forearm under the calf and the elbow under the knee, because he had one hand for that and the other was holding the frame off the boy while it tipped. The rail dragged. The leg went through an angle it had not gone through since the gravel bar, and Tonk screamed — the raw full scream, the one Jab had heard twice in his life and would hear in his sleep for the rest of it — and kept screaming while Jab got both arms under him and lifted him bodily off the ground and put him down in the angle of the wall on the dead grass.

"I know. I know. I know."

He got the hide out from under the frame's cover and got it round him. He got the blanket. He wedged the frame itself on its side across the road side of the hollow, lattice outward, which was not a wall but was a thing that would have to be come over or come round. That left the south side open — four feet of dead grass between the end of the frame and the bank, and nothing in it at all.

Then he took the twelve-foot cord off his own left wrist, where it had been tied every night for six days, and put the end of it into his brother's fist and closed the fingers over it.

"Cord," he said.

Tonk was sobbing and could not talk.

"Cord. Look at it. You pull that and I'm there. That's all it's ever meant and it means it now." He got the wooden fish off the stone where it had fallen and put it into the other hand, and Tonk's fingers closed on it so hard the knuckles went white. "Now. Here's your job, and it's a real one."

"I don't *want* a job —"

"There's people down the road with lamps. Four hundred paces. When you see them coming you shout, and you shout the whole time and you don't stop, and don't you dare stop because you think I'm busy." Jab put his forehead against his brother's forehead for exactly as long as he could afford, which was one second. "Arms in. Eyes wherever you like."

Then he stood up in front of the corner with the cord's other end tied round nothing at all, and turned round, and brought the light back up along the edge of the blade.

Grip outward, amber, thin. The gutter at the belly, one breath of it, guttering and catching. Then the whole edge lit and the hook at the end.

There were five of them on the road and they were much closer than they had been.

---

They had come up while he was on his knees. Of course they had. He had spent ninety seconds facing a wall with his hands full, and they had spent it moving, and now the nearest was eighteen feet away on the crown of the road and the rest were spread out behind and beside it in a loose fan that had the culvert head at the middle of it.

None of them was looking at him. They were all looking *past* him, at the corner, at the small shape in the dark behind his legs, and Jab understood with total clarity that they had not been counting him at all. They had been counting the thing he was carrying. They had been counting it for two miles, up a road, by the smell of an open wound and a fever at the top of its arc.

Something happened in his chest that was not fear, although the fear was still there underneath it, running.

"No," he said.

He stepped forward off the corner.

That was the first thing he got wrong, and it was a reasonable thing to get wrong, and it nearly finished both of them.

Here was his reasoning, and it had four years of use behind it. The metal was a tool. Dark, it was a heavy blunt wedge that would break a rope and split a log and take three strokes to get through the wet outside of a standing dead spruce. Lit, it went through green willow like nothing at all, and had once opened a jam that would have taken six people a day. Lit was what made it dangerous. Therefore, when a dangerous thing came at him, he should be the most dangerous he could possibly be, and he should be that out in front where it could be seen.

So he lit it wide and high and he took two steps out onto the road and put himself between the fan and the corner with the light up in front of him.

And the whole fan came off him like water off a plate.

---

It was not a retreat. That was what took him so long to understand, standing there with his blade out and his heart going. They did not back away and they did not break. They *slid* — the nearest one first and then the rest, all of them at the same speed, the way a flock of birds turns without anybody deciding — round to his right, away from the lit edge, out along the fan toward the open road and the top of the ditch.

Toward the south side.

Toward the four feet of dead grass the frame was not across.

Jab went after it. There was no thought in it. He turned and went right, with the blade out, and the fan simply went further right, and then the near one on the far end of the arc — the one he had stopped watching, because his eyes were full of the ones in front of him — put its head down and came.

It came fast and low and absolutely silent, and it did not come at Jab.

It went for the south side.

He got there because of the rail.

Not because of anything he did. He had spun to follow the fan and the frame was lying on its side in front of the corner with the curved ash rail standing up off it, and he took the rail across the shin, and falling forward put him four feet further right than any decision of his would have got him, and he came down on his left hand and one knee in the open grass with the lit blade held out sideways across his own body.

The hound went onto it.

He felt the whole event through the bones of his arm: not a cut, a *collision*, the shoulder of something heavy running onto a held edge at speed and the edge going in and the weight coming through after it. His arm folded. His elbow hit the stone. The blade came out somewhere and the hound went past him and into the frame and through it — through it, splitting the ash lattice with a crack like a shot — and Tonk shrieked in the corner.

Jab got his feet under him with no memory of deciding to.

The hound was turning in the four feet of space between the broken frame and the bank, turning badly, dragging its near foreleg, and its head was already coming round toward the corner and the small noise in it.

He went over it.

He did not have a technique. He had four years of putting a heavy blade through wet wood at awkward angles from bad footing, which was, it turned out, not nothing. He got his left hand into the scruff of the thing — it was like taking hold of a sack of hot sand that was moving — and put his weight onto it and drove the lit edge down and in behind the shoulder, using the belly of the shape, the fat part, the part his father had explained to him on a gravel bar, *it's for when you can't get behind a thing and you have to cut it from the side*.

It came apart.

Not the way a deer came apart. It came apart *all at once and from everywhere*, the entire animal letting go of being an animal, and what had been a hundred and something pounds of something under his hands was a grey blowing weight of ash that went through his fingers and up his sleeves and into his mouth and eyes, and Jab knelt in the gap in a cloud of it, coughing, retching, with the blade still lit in his fist.

"JAB —"

"Here!" He could not see. "I'm here. I'm here. Don't move."

He got one eye clear with the back of his wrist and dragged himself backward out of the open grass, into the angle, until his shoulder found stone.

There were four hounds standing on the road, spread across the crown at the lip of the dip, absolutely still, side-on, heads low.

They had not moved at all. Not one of them had come in behind the first. They had stood there and watched the thing happen from twenty feet away, and now they were doing the other thing, the stillness, the thing the first one had done in the ditch at thirty feet and he had thought was hesitation.

And behind them, out at the edge of the amber, a fifth one that he had lost the count of stood on the shoulder with its head up.

---

His left forearm had a hole in it.

He noticed that in the flat way you notice a thing during, rather than after: there was a warm wet weight in the sleeve and his fingers had gone slow. The scruff. It had got its head round while he had hold of it. He had not felt any of it happen.

His right hand was still holding the blade and the light was still on and the light was costing him and he could feel exactly what it was costing, which was the last of a bucket that had been nearly empty since a night in the snow.

He got up and put himself in the open south side.

That was the whole of the plan and it took him about a second to arrive at, and it was the only ground on the road that mattered: four feet of dead grass with the bank at his left shoulder, the broken frame at his right, and his brother in the angle of the wall behind his heels. He stood in it with the blade lit and his left arm hanging and four hounds standing out on the crown of the road in front of him, and he had no idea at all what to do next.

"Tonk. Are you in one piece."

"It *broke* the frame —"

"Are you in one piece."

"*Yes* —"

"Good. Shout now."

"What?"

"The people," said Jab. "The lamps. Shout for them. Shout now and don't stop."

And his brother, four years old, lying on broken stone in the angle of a culvert wall with a wooden fish in one fist and a cord in the other, filled his lungs and shouted — a high, cracked, furious noise that went out down a straight road under a black sky and did not sound like a boy at all.

The four hounds did not move.

And then, from somewhere down the road in the dark — not from the lamps, from much nearer than the lamps, off the shoulder where there was nothing to see at all — a girl's voice came back. It was flat and hard and it was not shouting at him. It was shouting at somebody a long way behind her, and every word of it arrived clean along the cold still air of the road bed:

"*That's the light! That's where they've gone! Five went past me up the west ditch four minutes ago and I said so twice!*"

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-01-hobbs-wall/chapters/chapter-27.md

# Chapter Twenty-Seven

The gate took twenty-six minutes, and Toren could not decide whether that was cruelty or just what happened to a place above a certain size — whether it stopped having the sort of insides where the difference could still be found.

They counted it because Dessa counted it. She sat down on the wet road at the ninety-foot mark with her leg out and her boots full of ash and said, "Right, I'm timing this," in a voice with something badly wrong in it, and then she timed it, out loud, in minutes, the whole way.

At eleven minutes a second lamp came on over the gate.

At nineteen a man came up onto the berm who had not been there before, in a long coat, with nothing in his hands, and looked at them for some time and then went away again.

At twenty-six the gate opened, and what came out of it was not help.

Four people came out in sealed grey coats with the hoods up and the faces behind flat panels, moving at a fast walk in a line abreast, and they went straight past the tailgate with Senna Voss on it and straight past a boy sitting in the road who could not stand up, and they went to Dessa's cooking pot.

"Ma'am, I need you to step away from that."

Dessa looked up at a panel with her own face in it.

"It's *ash*," she said.

"Yes, ma'am."

"It's eleven pounds of lifted ash, it's — there's four hundred pounds more of it in a road three miles back, there's a *year* of it out there, it's—"

"Yes, ma'am, and I need you to step away from it, and then I need your boots."

---

They took her boots. They took the pot and Marta Faust's flask and Rook's flask, and put all of it into a lidded bin with a clamp on it, and hosed the six of them down where they stood, in the rain, in the road, including the old woman on the board, who bore it without one word and afterward remarked that it was at least warm, which was true and which nobody else had noticed.

Then, and only then, two of them went back through the gate and came out again with a stretcher, and did the thing that Toren had been walking three hundred miles toward for four months, which was that a woman in a grey coat knelt down in a road beside Senna Voss and put a hand on her wrist and said, "All right, my love. What year did it happen?"

And Senna said, "2130."

"Where were you?"

"Inside the fence line. Eleven days."

"Which fence line?"

"The old plant at Caul Hill. I was — " she took the breath she had to go some distance for " — I was fifty-three and I went in for the copper, and I knew what was in there, and so did every other soul on that crew, and we went in anyway because it was copper."

The woman in the grey coat wrote it down. Then she said, "You've been carried a long way, haven't you," and Senna said, "By a great many people who could have done something else," and that was the last thing Toren heard her say for two days.

They took her through the gate at a fast walk with the stretcher level and one of them holding a bag of something up above her in the rain, and the gate shut behind them, and Toren sat down in the road in the shadow of a green berm and put his forehead on his one good arm and made a sound he was not proud of.

"That's it," said Rook, from where he was sitting. "That's the whole of it. That's all of it done."

"They took her—"

"They took her *in*, and there were proper instruments in that woman's hands, and she asked the right question first and she asked it in the right order, and I have been frightened for four months that this place wouldn't be what it said it was." Rook had his head back against the berm and his eyes shut and the rain going onto his face. "It's what it said it was. Everything that happens from here is a different kind of problem and I'll take every single one of them."

---

The reception yard was a pen of poured concrete forty feet by twenty with a drain in the middle and a bench along one wall and a hatch in the far end with a window in it.

They were in it for two hours and it was the warmest two hours any of them had had in a fortnight, because there were pipes running along the wall behind the bench, and the pipes were hot, and nobody had told them the pipes would be hot. Wyck put his back against them and shut his eyes and said, "Oh," in a completely undefended voice, and did not speak again for twenty minutes.

There was a light on in that yard. A real one, in a fitting, behind a cage, on a wire — a small flat white light that did not move and did not flicker and had no flame in it at all, and Toren looked at it for a long time with his arm in a sling somebody had given him, and thought about a road eleven miles south of Sallow Creek four months and a lifetime ago, and two boys arguing about whether the lights at Meridian were in the streets or only in the buildings.

"Wyck."

"I've seen it."

"You said only in the buildings."

"I'm aware of what I said," said Wyck Tanner, with his eyes shut and his back against a hot pipe. "I'd like to point out that this *is* a building."

"It's a yard."

"It's got a roof at that end."

At the far end there was a hatch, and behind the hatch was a room with more light in it, and in the room was a man at a desk with a book.

His name was Ivor Hallet. He gave it at once, unprompted, the way you give a thing that is going to be on a document anyway, and he was about forty-five, and he had a grey cardigan on under an oilskin and a pen on a string round his neck, and he was not unkind at any point in the next two hours, and Toren disliked him with a clean and total intensity for approximately eleven years.

"Right," said Ivor Hallet, through the window. "I'm going to do this in the order it's got to be done in, and I'd ask you to let me, because if I do it out of order I have to start it again and that's another hour for all of us. Persons in party."

Nobody said anything.

"Persons in party," said Ivor Hallet, pleasantly. "How many of you are there."

"Five," said Toren.

"Five."

"Five," said Toren again, and heard his own voice do something, and Dessa put her hand flat on his back between the shoulder blades the way Senna did, and left it there.

Hallet's pen stopped for about a second and a half. He did not ask. He wrote *five*, and went on, and Toren understood dimly that the man had seen that particular pause on that particular question a very great number of times.

"Names and ages, starting at your left. Names as you'd want them written."

"Toren Voss. Thirteen."

"Dessa Cole. Fifteen."

"Wyck Tanner. Fifteen."

Rook said, "Rook."

"That's not a name, sir."

"It's the one I've used for eleven years."

"It's the one you'll be called," said Hallet. "It isn't the one I can write. This is not a trick and I'm not asking you for a reason. I've got a book and the book has got a column and there's nothing in it I can put *Rook* in."

There was a silence in the reception yard that went on a little too long.

"Elias Marsh," said Rook.

Ivor Hallet wrote it down. Then he stopped writing. Then he turned back a very large number of pages in a very large book, and went down a column with the pen held above the paper the way a man does when he already knows, and found the place, and looked at it.

"Elias Marsh," he said.

"Yes."

"Out of Lowry Bend."

"Yes."

"You were at this gate on the fourth of the twelfth month." He gave the year, and it was eleven years ago. "You were nineteen and you had no fixed settlement and you gave a statement over two days concerning a research station and a company of men, and you were issued a transient card and a docket number, and the file says *referred*." He looked up. "Do you want to know what it was referred to?"

"No," said Rook.

"That's your right."

"I found out at the time," said Rook. "I stood at a window at the top of a stair for four hours on the second day and a very polite young woman told me the word means it's gone to the right place. And it had. That's the part nobody ever believes. It had gone to precisely the right place and it is still there." He had not moved and his voice had not changed at all. "Write the name. I've no quarrel with you, Mr Hallet, and I'd like that in the book as well if you keep a column for it."

"I don't," said Ivor Hallet, "and I would if I could."

---

Then they did the other thing, and the other thing took an hour.

"Declaration of capability," said Hallet, and laid four buff cards on the ledge inside the window, and did not push them through. "This is where I'm required to tell you exactly what this is before you fill one in, and I'm required to tell you that you may decline. So. Meridian keeps a register of Kindled persons inside the line. It's not a secret register, you can go and look at your own entry any Tuesday, and there's about eleven hundred names in it. If you're Kindled and you're inside this berm, you're expected to be on it.

"What goes on the card is your name, your rank if you've a word for it, and a plain description of what you can actually do. Not what you'd like to do. What you'd do at four in the morning if a wall came down.

"What you get for it is a residence band with a red thread in it, which gets you the same as everybody else's band and also gets you through the north yard, which nobody else can use, and which is where the work is.

"What it costs you is that you notify. If you go up a rank you tell this window within seven days. If you leave the line you tell this window. If you're going to do anything on a public street that a person who isn't Kindled would find remarkable, you tell this window first, and I'll tell you honestly that nobody does, and I'll tell you equally honestly that the two times in nine years that somebody really needed to have done, it went extremely poorly for them at a hearing."

"And if we decline," said Dessa.

"Then you're transient, and transients get fourteen days conditional and a grey band and no north yard, and there's a review, and I don't sit on the review and I've never been told how it goes."

"That's not a choice," said Dessa. "That's the same thing said twice with a bad face on one of them."

"Yes, miss," said Ivor Hallet. "It is. I'd write that down too if there were a column for it."

Wyck said, "Does she get treated either way?"

Hallet looked at him.

"The old woman," said Wyck. "On the stretcher. Does she get treated either way, or does she get treated if we sign these."

"She gets treated either way," said Ivor Hallet, and something came into his voice for the first time. "Her admission's a clinical matter and it went through at the gate and it is nothing to do with this window and nothing to do with any of you, and if anybody in this station ever tells you different, you will come back down here and you will say that to me, and I will make it the worst fortnight of that person's life. That's not procedure. That's mine."

"Right," said Wyck.

He took a card.

---

They filled them in on the bench with the pipes hot behind them and a paper cup of tea each, which was the first tea any of them had had that had not been made in a road.

Wyck wrote for a long time and then crossed most of it out.

"What do I put," he said.

"What you do."

"I don't *do* anything, that's the whole of the — " He stopped. He looked at his own strapped hand, and flexed the three fingers that could not feel anything, which he had done four hundred times a day for four months. Then he wrote one line and turned the card over so nobody could read it, and only years afterward, in a different room, did Toren find out that it had said *I don't fall over*, in Wyck Tanner's flat square hand, with nothing else on the card at all.

Dessa wrote hers in about nine seconds. *Dessa Cole. Ember. I know where people are, to about thirty feet, without looking, in the dark, all the time. It does not cost me anything.* She read it back once and added, at the bottom, *I limp,* which nobody had asked for and which she declined afterward to discuss.

Rook wrote four words and stopped.

Toren, sitting next to him with a sling on and a card on his knee, watched a man who had answered every question put to him for a month look at a buff card for the better part of a minute.

"Say it," said Toren.

Rook made a sound.

"You made me do it about nine hundred times," said Toren. "Say the whole question."

"The whole question," said Rook, "is whether I put down what I can do, which is the true answer and which will put a man in a coat in this yard within the hour, or whether I put down less, which is a lie in a book, and I have been telling that particular lie since I was nineteen years old and I have never once decided it was the wrong thing and I have never once felt clean about it either." He turned the pen over. "And the reason I can't do it this time is that I've spent a month telling four people to say the thing out loud even when it's awful, and one of them died still doing it, and I'm not going to sit in a concrete pen and be the only one who didn't."

He wrote it.

Toren did not see the card. He saw the length of time it took and he saw Ivor Hallet read it through the window and put it down and read it again, and then look up, over the top of it, through the glass, at a man sitting on a bench in a wet coat with a hole through his back.

"Mr Marsh," said Ivor Hallet.

"Yes."

"I'm going to have to have somebody else look at this."

"I know," said Rook. "Take your time. I'm not going anywhere at speed."

---

Toren's card said: *Toren Voss. Ember. One shape. It's out at present and I don't know when it's coming back.*

He looked at that for a while and then folded his hands over it, and Hallet, who missed nothing, said, "That's a true card and I'd rather have it than a good one," and took it, and did not ask.

Then Hallet turned to the last page of the day's sheet, and the tone of the room changed by about half a degree, and Toren — who had spent four months learning to read weather off people — felt it and did not understand it.

"Two more and then you can go and lie down," said Ivor Hallet. "These aren't from me. These came down from the yard office while you were on the bench, and I'm going to read them as they're written, because that's fairer than me putting them in my own words.

"One. *Has the party any knowledge of the Riftspawn presently ashed at grid marker south nine?*"

"We killed it," said Dessa.

Hallet wrote it. "*Two. Who did.*"

Nobody spoke.

Rook, on the bench, did not move and did not look at anybody, and it occurred to Toren afterward that the man had given them a rule in a road and had then sat in a concrete yard and let it run without touching it, because a rule you touch is not a rule.

"The question's got a name on it," said Ivor Hallet, after a moment. "I'll read that as well, because you'd have it out of me sooner or later. *The party reports a boy of thirteen. Confirm.*"

Toren put his hand flat on his knee.

"I did," he said.

The pen did not move.

"Say again?"

"I killed it," said Toren Voss. "I put an Ember into the root of its arm in a ditch beside the road at about the third hour this morning and it came apart. Wyck held the mule. Dessa called it from the tailgate with her eyes shut. He shut twelve feet of road across my grandmother twice and that's the only reason she's on your stretcher and not in that pasture." He found that his voice was perfectly level, which surprised him. "It's four hundred pounds of ash out there and you can go and weigh it and it's yours, and I'd like somebody to go and get the mule off the road, because he was nine hundred pounds of extremely disagreeable animal and he pulled that cart for three hundred miles and he has a name."

Ivor Hallet wrote all of it. It took him some time. He did not ask a single follow-up question, which Toren noticed, and which he did not understand for another four days, when it was explained to him that a man at an intake window does not ask follow-up questions about a thing that is going to be asked about again by somebody else.

Then Hallet put four paper bands on the ledge and pushed them through, and three of them had a red thread run through the paper.

"Conditional admittance," he said. "Fourteen days, reviewable, and the review's a formality in about nine cases out of ten. Yard C, second row, hut eleven — there's four beds in it and a stove and the stove works. The bathhouse is open till the tenth hour and there's no queue at this time of year because nobody comes in in the autumn.

"And there's three lights on the main street and they're on all night, and I'm told people find that hard to sleep next to at first, so put the blanket over the window and don't be proud about it."

---

Wyck stood in the middle of the main street of Meridian at about the eighth hour that night in a borrowed coat with his arm strapped across his chest and looked up at a light on a pole.

It was not much of a street. It ran two hundred yards between long low buildings with their roofs turfed over and their windows small and deep, and there was a drain down the middle of it, and there were three lamps on poles, and the light they put down was flat and white and steady and did not move at all, and under each of them a perfect circle of wet ground was lit up the colour of bone, and between them it was dark.

"It's three," said Wyck.

"It's three," Toren agreed.

"I said only in the buildings."

"You did."

"There's three of them," said Wyck, "in the street, in two hundred yards, and between them you can't see your hand, and that is not what either of us meant and I want that on the record."

"Nobody's keeping a record."

"Everybody in this place is keeping a record," said Wyck Tanner, "and I've been here four hours."

---

Toren went and stood outside the long building at the north end at about the eleventh hour, because Dessa had found out where it was and told him without being asked, which was the kindest thing anybody did for him that week.

He could not go in. There was a woman at a desk inside the doors who said, without any malice whatever, that visiting was in the afternoons, and that his grandmother had come in at the second hour with an exposure history of twenty years and was under Doctor Sowerby, and that Doctor Sowerby would speak to the family in the morning, and that no, she could not say more than that, and that yes, he could stand outside if he wanted to, and there was a bench.

So he stood outside, and then he sat on the bench, and the rain stopped at about the twelfth hour.

Rook came along the wall at some point after that. It took him a while to get there and he did not make anything of it, and he lowered himself down onto the bench in three separate movements and sat with his hands on his knees.

They were quiet for a bit.

"I answered it," said Toren.

"You did."

"You told us not to say anything."

"I told you not to say anything until somebody asked a question with a name attached to it," said Rook, "and a man in a window read your name off a sheet and asked you directly, and I sat nine feet away and let it happen, and I'd do it again." He rubbed his thumb once across his knee. "I'd have lied. I want you to know that, because you'll hear a version one day where I'm wiser than I am. I had the lie ready. I'd have shaved it — *there was a man on the road, we came on it after* — and it would have held for about a fortnight and then it would have come apart in front of somebody who mattered, and the difference between what they think of us now and what they'd have thought of us then is a year of everybody's life."

"Then it was right."

"It was right and it was expensive," said Rook. "Both. It's nearly always both, and people who tell you otherwise are selling something.

"Because here's what's in that book now. There is an old woman in that building being treated, and there's four of us on a register, and somewhere behind us there's four hundred pounds of the best ash anybody at this wall has seen in ten years lying in a road that their own people won't go out to in the dark. And on a sheet in a drawer it says that the thing that made it was put down by a boy of thirteen who has been Ember since the spring." He looked up at the long dark roofline. "They're not frightened of what came out of the Sag, Toren. They've got a procedure for what comes out of the Sag. They've had one for sixty years."

The door of the long building opened and shut somewhere down the wall and somebody crossed the yard with a lamp and went in again.

"They'll be frightened of us," said Toren.

"They'll be *careful* about us," said Rook, "which is a colder thing and it lasts longer, and it'll be in writing, and it'll still be in writing in ten years when everybody who wrote it is dead." He got his breath. "I've been trying since the gate to think of something to tell you that isn't that, and I can't, so we'll have the last one instead, and then I'm going to go and lie down for four days.

"You got her here. That's done. Nobody can take it off you and it's not conditional on anything and it's not reviewable in a fortnight, and whatever it turns out to have cost — and it's going to cost, and I don't know the number yet either — it is the only thing any of us set out to do, and it is done, and you did it."

Toren sat on a bench outside a long low building with electric light coming out from under the door in a flat white line, with his left arm broken and his right hand strapped and the back of his leg stitched by a person who had used a needle made for it, and a dead stone in his coat pocket, and a cold disk on a boot-lace against his breastbone, and he could not think of one single thing to say.

So he did not say anything, and Rook did not say anything either, and the two of them sat there until the last lamp on the main street went off at the first hour, which Toren learned the next day it did every night, because Meridian turned its lights off at the first hour, and always had, and had a written reason for it.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-01-hobbs-wall/chapters/chapter-28.md

# Chapter Twenty-Eight

Doctor Sowerby said the worst thing first, on purpose, and Toren decided inside four minutes that he liked him better than anybody else he had met behind that wall.

He was small and bald and in a hurry, and he came into the ward at the ninth hour on the third day with a folder under his arm and four chairs already put out by somebody else, which meant the number of chairs had been decided in advance by a person who knew how many of them there were, and Toren noticed that and did not like it.

"Right," said Doctor Sowerby. "I'll do this in the order I always do it in, which is the bad thing, then the good thing, then the bill, and I've been told I do it too fast, so stop me.

"The bad thing is that we cannot fix her. I want that said in this room with her sitting up in it, because in about a fortnight somebody in a corridor is going to say the word *treated* to you and you'll hear *cured*, and then you'll be angry with me in six months and I'd rather be got out of the way now. There is damage in this woman that was done in eleven days in 2130 and it has had twenty years to go on being done, and it is not coming out. Her marrow is failing. It has been failing for about nine years and whatever was keeping her upright on that road was not medicine."

"It was a flask of road-ash and a man with a hole in his back," said Senna, from the bed.

"It was, and it should not have worked, and there's a paper in it for somebody who isn't me.

"The good thing. We can take out what's still in her that's still doing harm — that's a course, it takes about twenty weeks, it's unpleasant, and it works; we've run it two hundred and eleven times and I've got the numbers in this folder and you can read them. And we can hold the marrow up from outside. Not replace it. Hold it up. That's a transfusion and a course of something we make here, on a cycle, and the cycle is eleven days, and it doesn't stop.

"And with those two things, this woman is not going to die this winter, and without them she was going to die within about five weeks, and I'd have given you six."

Nobody in that room said anything at all.

"And then the bill," said Doctor Sowerby, "and I'm going to let her ask for it, because it's hers."

"Say it," said Senna Voss.

He said it. It took him about ninety seconds and he did not soften one word of it, and when he had finished he asked her whether she wanted him to stay in the room, and she said no, thank you, and he went, and the door shut, and the four of them sat on four chairs that somebody had counted out.

---

"Right," said Senna. "I'm going to say the whole of it once, properly, with all of you here, because that is the rule in this family and I did not make it and I'm not going to be the first one to get out of it.

"And then I should like nobody ever to say it for me again, in any room, to anybody, for the rest of my life. If somebody asks, you send them to me. I have a great deal of time now and very little to do with it.

"First. I'm going to live." She let that sit. "That's the biggest one and it's first and nobody in this room is going to take it off me by being sad in front of me before I've finished. Three days ago I was five weeks from dying in a cart, and I am not, and four children and a man carried me three hundred miles to make that true, and if any one of you looks at your boots while I'm saying this I shall be extremely rude.

"Second. I'm going to live *here*.

"Not for the winter. Not until I'm stronger. Here. There is a machine in a shed at the north end of this station and it is the only one of its kind that anybody in this room has ever heard of or is ever likely to, and I must be inside a mile of it every eleven days for the rest of my life. Eleven days. Not a fortnight, not a month, not if I'm careful. Eleven.

"Which means I will not see Sallow Creek again. I will not see Hobb's Wall, or that dreadful weighmaster I was extremely fond of, or the Level, or that boy's stones on the spur, ever, and none of you are to build me a chair on wheels and get clever about it, because it isn't the distance, it's the eleven days, and I've done the sums four times and so will you and we will all get the same answer.

"And I have been in a cart since the second of the month. I've crossed three hundred miles of the worst road in this country lying on my back being no use to anybody, and it's the only thing in my life I was ever actually any good at, and I have done it for the last time, and I should like that noticed by somebody, because it will not be in any of that man's folders."

Dessa said, "It's noticed."

"Thank you.

"Third, and this is the one I'd rather not, so I'm going to do it quickly." She put her right hand out of the blankets and laid it on top of them, palm up. "The course takes the nerves. Not all at once and not everywhere — it takes the hands and the feet, and it does not give them back, and it is not a small percentage of people, it is nearly everybody, and he showed me the number and then he made me say it back to him.

"So in about four months I will not be able to feel anything with these." She turned the hand over. "I'll be able to move them. I'll be able to pick things up, if I look at them while I'm doing it. I won't know I'm holding them.

"And I won't walk. I can walk now, poorly, as far as that door. In the spring I shall not, and that's the feet, and that one's simple and there's nothing to discuss about it."

Toren said, "Gran—"

"Don't do that face," said Senna. "Just say it."

"Is it worth it?"

"Yes," said Senna Voss, immediately, without any weighing at all. "Yes, it is, and I want you to hear how fast I said that, because you'll want it in about ten years when somebody you love is in a bed and you're doing the same arithmetic.

"I am seventy-three. I have had twenty years of something that was always going to finish me, and a man in a corridor has just offered me eight more and told me to my face that he's seen twelve and he's seen three, which he did not have to do. Eight years. I shall watch that one — " the hand moved a little toward Toren " — stop being thirteen. I shall watch that one — " toward Wyck " — be told what to do by somebody and not do it. I shall watch Dessa Cole run a room. That is not a consolation prize. That is the entire thing I have wanted since the morning we left Sallow Creek, and they are going to sell it to me for my hands and my feet and the rest of the road, and I'd have paid more, and I told him so, and he said everybody says that in the first week."

She stopped and got her breath.

"I've finished," she said. "Somebody go and find out about the tea in this place, because it is not good and I refuse to believe that's the best they can do."

---

Hut eleven had four beds in it and a stove and a window with a blanket over it and a floor of poured concrete with a drain in the corner that Dessa filled with a rag on the second night because she said she could hear the station's insides through it.

That was the clan now. Four beds and a stove and a woman in a long building four hundred yards away, and no cart, and no mule, and no road.

Wyck slept eleven hours the first night and woke up at the eighth hour in a rage about it and had to be talked down by Dessa, who pointed out, in her flattest voice, that there was nothing whatever to keep watch against inside a berm with a gate on it and a man paid to stand at the gate, and that he had not been on watch for four nights and the world had continued.

"That's not the point."

"No," Dessa agreed. "It isn't."

He got a new bar on the fourth day. He went to the yard office and asked whether there was work and was told there was always work, and came back with eighteen inches of square-section steel out of a scrap bin that a man had cut down for him for nothing, and he spent the next four evenings sitting on the step of hut eleven turning it over in his left hand, poorly, dropping it, and picking it up, and dropping it.

"Say it," said Toren, on the third evening.

"Say what."

"You've been about to say something for an hour."

Wyck turned the bar over. He dropped it. He picked it up.

"He said he'd go," he said.

Toren did not say anything.

"At the fire on the lane. He said *I'll walk you to Meridian, and when you're through the gate and she's in front of somebody with proper instruments, I'll go.* He said it to all of us and I wrote it down in my head at the time because I write down everything that man says and so does everybody in this hut, and we are through the gate, and she is in front of instruments, and he's had nine days and he hasn't said a word about it, and I'm not going to sit here for a month waiting for it to happen at four in the morning."

"Then ask him."

"I'm not asking him," said Wyck Tanner. "If I ask him he answers, and then I'll never know whether he'd have said it on his own."

---

He said it on his own on the sixth day, in the hut, at the eleventh hour, with the stove going and all of them in the room — which meant that Dessa had gone and got Senna in a chair with wheels on it from the ward, over four hundred yards of wet path, with Wyck pushing and complaining, on the basis of a message that said *hut eleven, tonight, all of us* and nothing else.

Rook stood with his back to the stove because he could not sit down and get up again in one evening.

"I'm going to do this poorly," he said, "and I'm going to do it in one go, because there's a rule in this room about all of us at once and nobody finding out second, and it was set by somebody in a lane four months ago and it is the best rule anybody has ever given me.

"On the lane I said I'd walk you to Meridian, and I said that when you were through the gate and she was in front of instruments, I'd go. I meant it entirely. I had it worked out. There was a version of this week in my head where I stood at that gate on the day she went in, and shook four hands, and went east, and I have been carrying that version about with me since before any of you were hurt and it has been a great comfort to me and I'd like to put it down now."

He stopped.

"I'm not going," he said.

Nobody moved.

"That's all of it. There's no second part. I'm not going, and there isn't a reason I can give you that would stand up in front of the man I was in that lane, and I've stopped needing one." He looked at the stove rather than at any of them. "I've taken work in the north yard. It's ash-lifting on the Sag line and it's the worst money in this station and nobody else will take it because you have to be able to hold a road shut, and I've signed for four seasons, which in this place is a thing you sign.

"And I'll say the other half of that out loud now, so that nobody has to work it out on their own at four in the morning. Her course is clinical. It went through at the gate and it is not conditional on one single thing any of the rest of us does, and the man at that window told me so to my face and made a promise about it he didn't have to make, and I believe him.

"But she has to be inside a mile of that shed every eleven days for the rest of her life. Which means she has to be inside this berm. And being inside this berm is residency, and residency in this station is *reviewed*, and a household gets reviewed on what the station reckons it's worth. So that's what I've signed for. Not for her medicine. For her address.

"And if anybody in this room wants to say *you said you'd go*, now's when, and I'll hear the whole of it standing up."

There was a long silence.

Then Wyck said, "Right."

And that was the whole of it, and the stove ticked, and Dessa put her head back against the wall and shut her eyes, and Senna Voss said, "Well," in a voice that had a great deal in it, "I shall need somebody to push this chair on Tuesdays."

---

Toren went to the record office on the seventh day.

Rook told him where it was without being asked and then made a point of not coming.

"It's a long room at the top of the stair in the yard office and it's open to residents on Tuesdays and Fridays and the woman who runs it is called Annice Fell and she's been in that room longer than I've been alive. That's all I've got and it's all I'm giving you." He was sitting on the step with his coat over his knees. "I'm not going to tell you to do it. I'm not going to tell you not to. Your father gave you a rule in a road on the day he died and it was a good rule and I've told you to keep it twice, and I'm not going to be the man who talks you out of your father's last instruction in a yard in the rain. It's yours. You'll do what you do."

"What would you do?"

"I'd have done it eleven years ago if I'd had one," said Rook.

---

The record office was ninety feet long and smelled of dust and warm metal and had a single window at the far end, and the whole of one wall was paper and the whole of the other was steel cabinets with handles, and in the middle there was a desk with a screen on it that glowed a flat green and made a low noise that Toren could feel in his teeth.

Annice Fell was about sixty and had a cardigan and half-glasses on a chain and did not look up for some time.

"Resident?"

"Toren Voss. Yard C, hut eleven. Conditional."

"Conditional's fine. What is it you want?"

"I want to know if there's anything in here about an object."

"Everything in here is about an object," said Annice Fell. "Try again, and try again with a shape and a material and a measurement, because that's how the depot lists went in and it's how they come out. The catalogue doesn't know stories. It knows grammes."

Toren stood at the desk.

He thought about a cut in a road four months ago and a man with his hands in his coat pockets walking at a pace the road had agreed to beforehand, and *under the shirt, always, you don't show it, not at a gate, not at a wall, not to a friend*, and *because I don't know why, and neither did any of them, and we all did it anyway, and we're all still here, so we keep doing it*.

And he thought: *we're all still here* was said by a man who had nine days left.

He put his hand up under his collar and lifted the cord over his head and put the disk down on the desk.

It lay on the wood between them, dark, near-black, about the width of two thumbs, with nine fine grooves running from the centre out to the rim on the face that was showing, and nine on the other, on a cord of braided boot-lace gone soft with four months of being worn against a boy's chest.

Annice Fell looked at it for a moment. Then she got up and fetched a pair of callipers and a small scale, and she did not ask him one question about where it had come from, and Toren understood, watching her, that he had walked into the one room in Meridian where not being asked was the whole service.

---

"Thirty-one across," she said. "Thirty-one and a bit. Four millimetres. Forty grammes, which is wrong for that size in anything I can name. Non-ferrous — the magnet's doing nothing. Nine radial channels, both faces. Is that all nine? Count them for me, I don't trust my eyes on fine work."

"Nine."

"Nine," she said, and typed, and the green screen did nothing for a while, and then did something.

"Right," said Annice Fell. "Sit down."

Toren sat down.

"I'm going to tell you three things and then I'm going to tell you what I don't know, and I'd like you not to interrupt during the third one, because it's the one people interrupt during.

"First. There's one entry. Hardened Materials Depot, this station, receiving list, sixty-one years back, before the Fall's tenth anniversary and before there was any such thing as a Kindling. Item nineteen of forty-one, in a lot of returned survey stores with no manifest attached, which is itself unusual and which the clerk noted at the time in three words and then let go." She turned the screen a little, which was a courtesy, and which Toren could make nothing of. "Dimensions as I've just read them off your desk there. The clerk wrote them down sixty-one years ago and I wrote them down four minutes ago and they are the same to a tenth of a millimetre."

"So it's—"

"Second," said Annice Fell. "There's a note in the margin in a different hand and a different ink, pencil over ink, and it was written after the list was made, and I'm going to read it to you as it is, and I'd ask you to let me get to the end.

"*Not an instrument. Do not requisition. It came here in a gift's coat and it is not a gift; log it, shelf it, and do not let anybody wear it.*"

Toren sat absolutely still.

"There's more," said Annice Fell. "*It has already saved a world and it has already ended one. It will not do either again. Whoever is frightened of what this is going to do has not understood what it is.*"

She took her glasses off.

"That's the end of the note," she said. "There's no name on it. There's a mark where a name would go, and it isn't initials, it's a mark, and there are four of them in that ledger, all on the same two pages, and in thirty-one years I have never found a name attached to one and I have looked, because I am that sort of person."

---

Toren could hear the screen humming. It was the only thing in the room that was making any noise at all.

*A curse in a gift's coat. That's what my grandfather called it. He wore it forty years, and he never once let anyone see it.*

He heard it in his father's voice, exactly, the way you carry a hot thing across a room. And under it the other one, the warning, the thing that had survived when everything else in that family got repeated wrong for sixty years — *because warnings survive* — *it's going to save the world or it's going to end it, that's the whole thing, that's all anybody ever knew, and nobody who ever carried it lived long enough to find out which.*

His grandfather had not made up that phrase.

It had come with it. It had come in the box.

And whatever had actually been said, at the start, by whoever made that mark, had not been a warning about something that was going to happen. It had been a *report*. Somebody sixty-one years ago had written down that it was finished, and the Voss family — who had had it, Tam and Tam's mother and the sixty wrong years and Garrick's grandfather and Garrick and now a boy sitting in a chair in a long room — had spent five generations being frightened of the wrong tense.

"You're very pale," said Annice Fell.

"What's the third thing?"

"The third thing is the disposal line, and it's the reason I told you to sit down." She put her glasses back on. "That depot did not release material. That was the entire point of it — things went in and they were logged and they stayed in, and there is a column for release authorisations, and in sixty-one years of that ledger, across four thousand items, that column is empty. Empty. There is no such thing as a thing that came out of the Meridian hardened depot, and the reason I can say that with my whole chest is that I have read every page of it twice.

"Item nineteen is struck through. And in the authorisation column, in the same pencil as the note, it says: *released to bearer.*

"Not released to a name. Not released to a station, a company, a survey, a family. *To bearer.* No date. No signature. No countersign, no docket, no receipt, and there is no second entry anywhere in this room, from that year or any year, that ever mentions it again until a boy of thirteen put it down on my desk on a Tuesday."

---

Toren walked back across the station with the cord back round his neck and the disk cold under his shirt against his breastbone, exactly as cold as it had always been, and found Rook on the step of hut eleven with his coat over his knees, in the same place, which Toren was reasonably certain meant the man had been there for three hours.

He told him the whole of it. It took about four minutes and he got every word of the note right, because he had made Annice Fell read it to him twice and had then said it back to her until she stopped correcting him.

Rook listened to all of it with his head down.

Then he sat for a long time without saying anything, and the light on the main street came on, and a man went past the end of the row with a bucket.

"Say it," said Toren.

"No."

"You said ask you in about a year. It's not a year."

"It isn't," said Rook.

"Then say something, because I've been carrying it up that path for twenty minutes and it's got heavier every step and I don't know what to do with it."

Rook lifted his head.

"All right," he said. "One thing, and then I'm done tonight, and I'd like you to take it away and not open it in front of anybody.

"Your father told you a thing was going to happen. That paper says it already has." He was not looking at Toren. He was looking at the lit circle of wet ground under the lamp at the end of the row. "There is no version of that where what you've got round your neck is an object, Toren. Objects don't have a tense."

He got himself up off the step in three movements, with his hand on the door frame.

"Ask me again in a year," he said. "And I'll tell you what I believe, and it will be worse than tonight, and you'll be able to hold it by then. That's not me being careful with you. That's me not being ready, and I'd rather say that out loud than dress it up as looking after you, because a boy on a wall four months ago told me to say things out loud even when they're awful, and I have been extremely slow about learning it."

---

The card came on the ninth day.

It was buff, and it was the size of a hand, and a boy of about ten brought it to the door of hut eleven at the fourth hour and waited to be told there was no reply.

There were two lines on it.

*E. MARSH and T. VOSS are requested at the Director's office, second floor, yard office, on the twenty-ninth, at the second hour, concerning (i) the south approach incident of the nineteenth and (ii) a records consultation of the twenty-fifth. Attendance is not compulsory. Please bring your bands.*

Dessa read it over Toren's shoulder and then took it out of his hand and read it again.

"They've put them on the same card," she said.

"It's a card, Dessa, they've probably got one—"

"They have got more than one card," said Dessa Cole. "I have been in this station nine days and I have counted eleven kinds of paper. They put those two things on one card because somebody has sat at a desk and joined them up, and I'd like everybody to notice that the two things are *you killed something nobody can kill* and *you put a thing on a table in the record office*, and that until seven days ago the second one didn't exist."

Nobody said anything.

"*Attendance is not compulsory*," said Wyck, from the step, without looking round.

"Yes."

"They wrote that down." He turned the bar over in his left hand, and did not drop it. "That's the bit I'd want a column for."

---

They did the bread that night, because Dessa said the day had to have a halt in it, and because Senna was in the hut in the chair with the wheels on it and would have to go back at the tenth hour.

It was Meridian bread, which was grey and even and came in a wrapper with a number printed on it, and Dessa cut it poorly, in slabs, on the top of the stove-box, with a lot of concentration.

"I know the order," she said.

"I know you do," said Senna.

She picked up the end crust.

"Senna."

She put it in her grandmother's hand, and then, without being asked, she folded the fingers over it, because the course had started on the fourth day and by the ninth there was something wrong with the hand already, four months ahead of everything Doctor Sowerby had drawn on his folder — which happened sometimes, and which he had told them plainly happened sometimes, in the same voice he used for everything else.

Senna looked down at her own hand.

"It's there," said Dessa.

"I know it's there. I can feel the *weight* of it. I can't feel the bread." She turned the hand over slowly, watching it, and the crust stayed where it was. "It's like being handed things through a glove. A thick one. That's the whole of it and it isn't dramatic and I'd be obliged if nobody made a face."

"Do you want—"

"I do not," said Senna Voss, "want anybody in this room to hold my bread for me. Not tonight, not in the spring, not in eight years, and I'd like that minuted." She lifted it, the way you lift a thing you are doing with a tool. "I can see it perfectly well. I shall simply have to look at it, and I have looked at worse."

She ate the crust, looking at it.

"Me," said Dessa, and put a slab down beside her own hand and did not eat it.

"Wyck."

Wyck took it, left-handed.

"Rook."

Rook took it.

"Tor."

Toren took it.

"Milo," said Dessa, and cut the last slab, and put it on the cloth at the end of the row, and folded the cloth over it.

Then she did the thing she had not done on the bank at the Level, and had not done on any night since, and had plainly decided on the path over from the ward with Wyck complaining behind her.

"He'd have hated this place," she said.

Wyck made a noise.

"He would. He'd have hated it for about four days and then he'd have known everybody's name in the whole north yard and he'd have been telling that Hallet man his business, and he'd have got a lamp — he'd have got one of those lamps off the pole somehow, he'd have found out who owns them —" She stopped, and put the knife down, and carried on anyway, in the manner of somebody walking over a bad patch of road without slowing down. "He'd have stood in the middle of that street with three lights on it and said something completely idiotic about how it wasn't as good as his."

"It wasn't as good as his," said Wyck.

"It wasn't," Rook agreed.

"It was *terrible*," said Dessa. "It was a jar of fireflies. It was never once enough to see by in four months and it is the only thing that has ever got any of us anywhere."

Senna Voss put her right hand flat on the top of the stove-box, which was warm, and which she could only just tell was warm, and would not be able to tell by the spring, and left it there anyway.

"Milo Faust," she said, to the room, in a voice like a dry stick breaking. "There. Now it's been said tonight, and it'll be said tomorrow, and I shall say it every single night in this station until they carry me out of it, and none of you have to."

---

Toren went out and sat on the step at about the twelfth hour, after Wyck had pushed the chair back across to the long building and come back, after the stove had been banked, after the last of them had stopped talking.

The lamp at the end of the row went out at the first hour, the way it did.

He sat in the dark with his back against the door of a hut in a place with a written reason for everything, and put his hand in his coat pocket, the way he had done about every forty steps for four months without ever quite deciding to.

He had stopped checking after the fourth day. That was the honest truth of it and he had told nobody: he had checked it nine times a day for four days and found a stone, and on the fifth day he had made himself stop, because a man on a towpath in the spring had told him what checking on it did, and because he had been told there was one girl's worth of evidence in the whole world and no more.

So he had not gone looking. He had gone eleven days and had not gone looking once.

It was warm.

Not hot, and not much, and not steady — it came and went under his fingers like something breathing, exactly the way it had on four hundred yards of canal towpath with a sack of corn across his knees and a boy calling imaginary husks at his back. It was the least impressive thing anybody had ever felt.

Toren Voss sat on a concrete step at the first hour with his broken arm in a sling and a summons on the table behind him and his grandmother in a building four hundred yards away with a pair of hands that were already going, in a station that had written every one of them down in a book, and held a warm stone in his coat pocket, and did not take it out, and did not look at it, and did not ask it for anything at all.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-02-meridian/source-pool/callie/chapter-18.md

# Chapter Eighteen

They gave her the hook back at the hatch in the morning, and it was clean.

Callie stood at the window in the reception yard and held it in both hands and did not know what to do with it. The grey coats had washed it. They had washed it the way they had washed the party in the road, all over, with warm water and something that smelled sharp. The black smear of old husk down the inside of the curve was gone. It had been there since the culvert on the first night, and since long before that, since the hayfield. It had been there so long she had stopped seeing it. Now it was gone and the steel underneath was grey and bright, and the edge she had put on it with a wet stone at Gaunt's Reach shone along the inside of the curve like a line of water.

The tag was still on the handle. It had her name on it in somebody's neat hand, and a number.

"It's yours," said the man at the hatch. It was not Hallet. It was a younger man with a cough. "Sign there."

She signed. She took the tag off the handle and put it in her coat, and she did not know why she kept it.

---

The board was in the yard office, on the wall of a long room with a queue of nobody in it. It was a sheet of paper as big as a door, ruled in columns, with names down the side in a clerk's hand and numbers across.

Katori's name had the biggest number on it that Callie had ever seen written next to a person. Pell stood in front of it for a long time with his hands in his pockets.

"Well," he said at last. "That's a winter."

"That's a winter for five," said Katori. She did not sound pleased. She sounded like somebody reading a distance. "With the stable fee, and the huts, and whatever they'll want for the horse. And nothing for the road back."

"There's nothing for the road back anyway," said Pell. "The man at the stable says nobody goes out on the high road after the first frost. He says it like a law."

"Is it a law?"

"He didn't say it was a law. He said it like one." Pell looked at the board. "I asked about a team, for the bridge. He laughed. It wasn't unkind. He just laughed."

Katori said nothing. Callie watched her look at the number beside her name, and knew she was thinking about eleven crates under a net under a concrete pier, a long way south on a plain with hounds on it.

Callie's own name was lower down. There was her tenth of the four hound Flasks, which had been more than she expected. And there were three Handfuls, lifted, clean, in her mother's name and hers. Beside that there was a small number in another column, marked *less station*, and beside that a smaller number that was what was left.

"That's the jar," she said.

"That's the jar," said Pell. "Less what they take for weighing it. They always take something for weighing it."

Callie looked at it. Four hundred miles, and a spring, and a culvert on the first night. It was not a number that would fill a column. It was hardly a number at all.

"It's in the book," said Pell, beside her. "Theirs. And mine. It's not nothing."

"It's nearly nothing."

"Nearly nothing's a different thing from nothing," said Pell. "I've been saying that to Otto for three years as well."

---

Visiting at the long building was in the afternoon. Callie went in the morning anyway.

The long building was at the north end of the station, past the main street with its three lamps on poles, all dark now in the grey daylight. It was long and low like everything else here, with a turf roof and small deep windows, and it went on for a long way. There were doors at one end, and a woman at a desk inside them.

"Visiting's in the afternoons," the woman said. She said it without any malice whatever. "Your mother came in yesterday morning. She's had a good night. Doctor Sowerby saw her last thing, and he'll see her again, and there's a picture being taken today. He'll speak to the family tomorrow."

"What picture?"

"Of the inside of her head," said the woman. "It's a machine. It doesn't hurt. It's loud." She looked at Callie. "You can wait outside if you want. There's a bench."

So Callie went out and sat on the bench.

It was against the long wall of the building in a strip of pale sun. She sat on it with the hook across her knees and her left arm against her ribs and looked at the yard in front of her, which was poured concrete, with a drain.

She had not been sitting there long when somebody came along the wall and stopped.

It was the boy from the step. He was taller than she had thought in the dark, about her height, with his left arm in a sling across his chest and his right hand wrapped in white, and he stood a little crooked, as if the back of one leg did not like being straightened. He looked at her, and at the bench, and at the hook across her knees.

"They told you it's afternoons," he said.

"Yes."

"They told me that the first night. I sat out here till the first hour." He looked at the bench. "Can I — "

She moved the hook. He sat down on the other end of the bench, slowly, in two movements, the way somebody sits who has learned where everything hurts.

"Toren," he said.

"Callie."

"You're in fourteen."

"You're in eleven."

That seemed to be all either of them had. They sat on the bench in the strip of sun and looked at the drain.

---

"You read that off the grass," said Toren, after a while. "Last night. The mule. You knew it was a mule from the size of the — "

"From the size of the mound." Callie looked at the drain. "And I read the other thing too. Before that. On the big road."

He went very still.

She had not meant to say it. She said it anyway, because it was the thing between them on the bench and she did not know how to sit beside it and not say it.

"Something came across the pasture from the east," she said. "Heavy. Three legs to a stride, not four. Wide, the way a thing stands that's made for pushing. And a fourth one that wasn't a leg. It cut the grass off level, like mowing, and it cut a line in the turf a man long, clean as a knife through a loaf." She kept her eyes on the drain. "It hit the rail on the east side and bent it. It went on up the grass to the ditch. And somebody was in the ditch. Under it. There's a hollow at the edge the size of somebody lying down." She stopped. "And it's dead, and it's been lifted. Somebody killed it."

Toren did not say anything for a long time.

"You got all of that," he said at last, "off *grass*."

"It's what I do. It's the only thing I'm good at." She turned the hook over on her knees. "I'm not asking who. I'm not asking anything. I just read it, and you were sitting here, and I didn't want to sit here knowing it and you not knowing I knew."

He looked down at his strapped hand.

"Yes," he said. That was all.

She did not ask. She knew about not asking. Katori had taught her, and Pell had taught her, and her mother had been teaching her since she was four. She sat on the bench and let the *yes* be the whole of it, and after a while she felt him let his breath out beside her, very slowly, as if he had been holding it since the gate.

---

He put his right hand in his coat pocket. Then he took it out again.

He did it the way you do a thing you have done so many times that your hand has stopped asking you. It went into the pocket and stopped there, and then, as if it had remembered something, it came out again and lay on his knee, and he looked at the drain.

Callie watched him do it. She knew that movement. She knew it in her own hands.

"I did that," she said. "With my hook. On a hill. I had my hand on it and I kept looking at it, to see if it was still there. And every time I looked, it went."

Toren turned his head and looked at her.

"Somebody told me that," he said slowly. "On a towpath. That checking on it's what puts it out."

"Somebody told me something like it. After." Callie looked at her own hands on the hook. "She said you can stop holding a thing, and it goes on being held. She said she didn't know that. She said she'd been holding things very hard for a long time."

He was quiet.

"It's out," he said. It came out of him low and flat, the way a thing comes out that has been sitting in a person for a long time with nobody to say it to. "Mine. My Ember. It's been out since the ditch. It's a stone. I don't know when it's coming back."

Callie thought about the pump house, and the gravel behind it at dusk, and Katori sitting down beside her.

"It's not gone," she said. "It's empty. Those are different."

"How do you know?"

"Somebody told me. When mine was." She looked at him. "I'm dry now. Nearly dry. I did too much on the plain. And it's coming back, slowly, on its own. I can feel it at the bottom. You can't use it for anything. You just know it's there." She thought about it. "Hers was nine days, once. The second time she emptied herself. She said she was sure it was gone. Absolutely sure."

Toren looked at his coat pocket for a long moment. He did not put his hand in it.

"Nine days," he said.

"That was her. I don't know about you. I don't know anything about yours." She stood up, slowly, because the door of the long building had opened and the woman from the desk was looking out at them. "I'm only telling you what somebody told me. Because it helped."

---

The ward was a long room with beds down both sides and a window over every bed, and it smelled of soap and something sharp, and there was a light in the ceiling that did not flicker.

Her mother was in the fourth bed on the left, under the window.

She was lying on her back in a white bed with white sheets folded down over a grey blanket, and her hair was loose on the pillow, out of Ines's braid. Somebody had washed it. Somebody had put her in a white gown with strings at the back. She looked smaller in the bed than she had ever looked on the wagon, and cleaner, and much more tired, and when Callie came down the ward she turned her head on the pillow and looked at her and her whole face changed.

"There you are," she said.

"Here I am."

"They washed my hair." Her mother's voice was thick and slow, the way it had been the morning after the hill. The good day was spent. It had gone out of her some time in the night, in this bed. "They washed all of me. They put a needle in my arm and a bag on a hook. And they asked me my name eleven times, and what year it was, and who was in charge of the station. I didn't know who was in charge of the station. I told them I'd find out and let them know."

"Did they laugh?"

"One of them did. The young one." Her mother's left hand moved on the blanket, and Callie sat down on the chair by the bed and took it. "The doctor came last night. Small man. Bald. In a hurry. He said he'd do it in order, the bad thing first, and then he didn't do anything at all, because he said he didn't know what the bad thing was yet. He said he'd need a picture." She looked at the ceiling. "I liked him. I liked him for not guessing."

"They're taking the picture today."

"So I'm told. Of the inside of my head." Her mother's mouth moved. "I'd be interested to see it. I've been wondering what's going on in there since the spring."

---

"You're the daughter," said a voice from across the ward.

Callie turned.

In the bed opposite, under the window on the other side, there was an old woman sitting up against her pillows. She was very thin and very straight, with white hair cut short and a long face that had been out in a great deal of weather. She had a tray across her lap with a cup on it and a plate, and on the plate there was a piece of bread. She was looking at Callie with a pair of pale eyes that did not seem to miss anything.

"Your mother walked in," the old woman said. "Through the south gate. On her own feet, with the gate nurse at her elbow. They were talking about it at the desk this morning, which they never do. Nobody walks in. They come in on a board." She looked at Callie's mother. "I came in on a board. I'd have walked if I could. I'd like that noted."

"It's noted," said Callie's mother, from her pillow, without opening her eyes.

"Senna," said the old woman. "I'd get up, but I'm enjoying myself."

Callie's mother opened her eyes.

She turned her head on the pillow and looked across the ward at the old woman in the opposite bed, for a long moment. Callie watched something go across her mother's face that she had not seen there since Thistle Ford. It was not a smile. It was the look her mother got when she met somebody at a wall or a market who was going to be worth talking to.

"Tilda," said her mother. "I said that on the first morning. To a stranger. By a fire."

"Did you."

"Almost exactly."

"Then we've got that settled," said Senna, "and we can get on to the interesting part."

---

The interesting part, it turned out, was the bread.

Senna picked it up. She did it slowly, looking at her own hand the whole time. She watched her fingers go round the bread the way you watch somebody else's fingers do a thing you have told them to do. When she had it, she lifted it, still looking, and took a bite, and put it down again, and let go of it, looking.

Callie's mother watched her do it.

"Your hands," said her mother.

"They've started going," said Senna. She said it the way Pell said a number. "It's the treatment. It takes the hands and the feet, and it doesn't give them back. It's started early. I can feel the weight of a thing. I can't feel the thing." She looked across at Callie's mother's right hand, lying on the blanket the way a glove lies on a table. "And yours?"

"It's stopped taking instruction. Since the winter."

"Can you feel it?"

"I can feel it. I just can't tell it anything." Callie's mother looked at her own hand. "It's like shouting at somebody in the next field."

Senna was quiet for a moment. Then something happened to her long weathered face that made her look about thirty years younger.

"Well," she said. "Which of us is going to cut the other's bread?"

"Neither," said Callie's mother at once. "We'll look at it."

Senna laughed. It was a short dry sound like a stick breaking. "I've been saying that to four people and a man for a week," she said, "and not one of them has laughed."

"They're frightened of you," said Callie's mother.

"They are. I've worked very hard at it."

"I'll need to know how you do it," said Callie's mother. "I've got five of my own and none of them are frightened of me at all. The horse is. The horse is the only one."

---

Toren came in at the start of the afternoon, when the woman at the desk let the visitors through. He came down the ward and stopped at the foot of Senna's bed, and then he saw Callie, sitting by the bed across from it, and stopped again.

Senna looked from one of them to the other. She did not seem to need anything explained to her.

"You two have met," she said.

"On a step," said Toren.

"On a step," Senna agreed, as if this were the only proper place for people to meet. "Tilda, this is my grandson. He is thirteen and he has a broken arm, and I'm going to live, and he's not going to cry about it in front of anybody, are you, Tor."

"No, Gran."

"Good." Senna turned her head on the pillow. "Tilda has a daughter who reads ground. That's what she said at the desk, apparently. The girl who came in with her. She reads ground. I thought you'd want to know that before she read yours."

"She already did," said Toren.

Senna looked at him. Then she looked at Callie, for a long time, with those pale eyes that did not miss anything.

"Did she," she said.

---
