They came for Callie's mother in the middle of the afternoon, two people in white with a bed on wheels.

"The picture," one of them said. "It takes about forty minutes. She has to lie very still. You can come as far as the line."

Callie did not know what the line was until she got to it. She walked beside the rolling bed, holding her mother's left hand, down the ward and out through a door and along a corridor that went down, a long slope under the building, where the air was cooler and the walls were thick and grey and there were no windows at all. At the bottom of the slope there was a heavy door, wider than the gate in the reception yard, and in front of the door there was a line painted across the floor. It was bright yellow, as wide as her hand.

On the wall beside it there was a sign.

*NO METAL PAST THIS LINE.*

Callie looked down at the hook in her other hand.

"It's the machine," said the one in white, seeing her look. "It doesn't like metal. It pulls. Anything iron that goes in that room, it'll have it off you and across the floor at it, and it won't ask." He looked at her mother. "Anything iron on you, ma'am? Pins? Rings?"

"Nothing," said her mother. "I sold the rings at the Reach."

He looked at Callie. "You'll have to wait here, miss. With that."

"I know."

Her mother's hand tightened on hers. It was the left one. It could still tighten.

"Forty minutes," said her mother. "I've lain still on a wagon for a month. I'll lie still in a room for forty minutes."

"I know."

"You'll be here."

"I'll be here."

They took her through the heavy door, and it closed behind her with a sound like a vault, and Callie stood at the yellow line with her grandfather's hook in her hand and nothing on the other side of the door that she could see or hear or reach.

There was a bench against the wall. She sat on it. She laid the hook across her knees.

Then, from somewhere behind the thick grey wall, very far off, the knocking started.

---

It was a loud sound, and a strange one. It came through the wall in long runs, like somebody hammering on a pipe in another building, fast and steady and mechanical. It stopped, and started again, faster, and stopped. It went on and on. It did not sound like anything that could look at the inside of a person.

Callie sat on the bench and listened to it. She could not see her mother. She could not hear her. She could not count her breaths through a wall as thick as a bank.

She held the hook.

She held it harder. She did not mean to. Her fingers closed round the conduit, the way they had closed round the curve at the top of the pitch, and she sat there gripping four feet of steel pipe as if it could do anything at all, as if holding it tight enough would put her on the other side of the door.

"There's somebody in there by herself," said a voice. "She's lying very still."

Callie looked up.

---

A girl was standing in the corridor at the top of the slope, pushing a chair with wheels on it. There was an old woman in the chair with a blanket over her knees. It was Senna.

The girl was about fifteen, dark, with a hard straight look to her. She stood with her weight all on one leg, the way somebody stands who has learned not to trust the other one. She was looking not at Callie but at the heavy door, with her head a little on one side.

"Dessa," said Senna, from the chair. "Don't frighten the child."

"I'm not frightening her. I'm telling her." The girl looked at Callie at last. "She's about twenty feet in. On her back. She hasn't moved. She's lying very still." She paused. "Is that your mother?"

"How do you — "

"I know where people are. About thirty feet, all round, without looking. It's not a trick." The girl said it flatly, like a thing she had said many times and was tired of having to say. "It doesn't cost me anything. It's just there." She looked at the door again. "She still hasn't moved. I can tell you if she does."

Callie sat on the bench with the hook across her knees and looked at her.

Her fingers had come open on the conduit. She had not told them to.

"Would you?" she said.

---

The girl came down the slope, pushing the chair, and stopped it beside the bench. She did it carefully. Callie could see the bad leg taking the weight of the slope and not liking it. Then she sat down on the other end of the bench, and stretched the leg out straight in front of her, and looked at the door.

"Dessa Cole," she said.

"Callie."

"I know. Senna told me. You read ground." Dessa did not look away from the door. "She's still not moving. She's very good at it."

"She's been practising," said Callie. "On a wagon. For a month."

"It shows."

Senna, in the chair, had shut her eyes. "We were on our way back from the shed," she said, without opening them. "They do things to me in the shed, and they did them today, and Dessa came to push me back, and then she stopped at the top of the slope and said *somebody's down there by herself*. And I said, Dessa, it's a hospital, everybody's somewhere by themselves. And she came down anyway." She opened her eyes. "Dessa Cole, sit up straight, you're making the corridor nervous."

"I'm sitting how I sit."

"You're sitting like somebody who counted ten kinds of paper between the hut and the ward."

"Eleven," said Dessa. "There's a new one on the ward door. It's green."

Callie looked at her.

"You count," she said.

Dessa turned her head.

"I count too," said Callie. "Everything. Husks. Seconds. The days to Meridian. The ash in the jar. My mother's breathing." She stopped. "I put it on my card. At the bottom. *I count.* I don't know why."

Dessa looked at her for a long time with that hard straight look.

"Good," she said at last. "Somebody in this place should." She turned back to the door. "Still hasn't moved. The knocking's stopped. Somebody's walking in there now. Two people. They're going to her."

The heavy door opened.

---

Her mother came out on the rolling bed with her eyes shut and her hair every which way on the pillow, and opened her eyes when they crossed the yellow line, and found Callie.

"It knocked," she said. Her voice was very tired. "Like a man mending a roof inside my head. I didn't care for it."

"You stayed still."

"I'm very good at staying still." Her mother turned her head and saw the chair, and the old woman in it, and the dark girl on the bench with her leg stuck out. "Oh. We've got company."

"You didn't move once," said Dessa. "The whole forty minutes. I was counting."

Callie's mother looked at her.

"I know," she said. "I could tell there was somebody counting." She shut her eyes again. "There usually is."

---

Doctor Sowerby would speak to the family in the morning. The woman at the desk told them so when they brought Callie's mother back up to the ward, and she said it the way you'd say a train was coming.

"At the ninth hour," said Callie's mother, from the pillow.

"At the ninth hour."

"I'd like chairs," said her mother. "Enough for all of them. There's five. And me."

The woman at the desk wrote it down.

When visiting was over, Callie stood by the bed and did not want to go. Her mother lay with her eyes shut and her left hand on the blanket. The bag on its hook dripped. Across the ward Senna was sitting up looking at a new piece of bread, and Toren was gone.

"Callie," said her mother, without opening her eyes.

"Yes."

"Tomorrow. Whatever he says." Her mother's hand moved on the blanket and found Callie's wrist. "You'll let me hear it first. Not you first and then you tell me, the way you tell Pell a road. Me first. It's my head."

"I promise."

"You've promised me a lot of things this week."

"I've kept all of them."

"You have," said her mother, and let go of her wrist. "Go on. Go and eat something. And somebody tell Pell to stop reading his book in the corridor. They've asked him twice."

---

The man in the long coat came to the door of hut fourteen at dusk.

Callie knew him at once. He was the man who had come up onto the top of the bank at fourteen minutes and looked down at all of them, and at her longest. He was tall and thin, about fifty, with a close-cut grey beard and a quiet face, and he had nothing in his hands but a folded paper. He stood on the step and knocked, and when Otto opened the door he asked for Miss Callie, by her whole name, the one off the scale-house door.

Callie came to the door. Katori came and stood behind her without being asked. So did Pell.

"I'm from the yard office," the man said. He did not give his name. "I've read your card."

"All of it?"

"All of it." He looked at her. "Starting with the first line. Mr Hallet said you'd asked him to tell whoever read it to start there. So I did." He paused. "*I read ground.* It's a good first line."

"It's the true one."

"I think they're all true," said the man. "That's why it came to me." He held up the folded paper, but did not give it to her. "We'd like you to come to the north yard. Not now. When you're not dry. You say on your card you're dry at present, and I believe you. When you've got something back in you. We'd like to see."

"See what?"

"What it says on the card." His face did not change. "The hook. The thing you can put in it. Nobody here's seen that, miss. Nobody I've ever met has seen that. We'd like to understand what we've got on the register."

Callie stood in the doorway. She felt Katori behind her not moving at all.

"My mother's got a doctor at the ninth hour tomorrow," she said. "I'm not doing anything till after that. Not anything."

"No," the man agreed at once. "Of course not." He put the folded paper back in his coat. "There's no hurry. Nobody's asking you to do anything tomorrow, or the day after. It's only a request. You can say no to it." He looked at her for a moment longer. "Most people don't."

He nodded to Katori, and to Pell, and went away down the row in the dusk, past the door of hut eleven, where a boy with an iron bar was sitting on the step turning it over in his left hand and did not look up.

Callie stood in the doorway until he was gone.

"Most people don't," said Pell quietly, behind her.

"I heard."

"That's the sort of thing," said Pell, "that I'd want a column for."

Callie went back in and sat down on the floor by the stove, with her grandfather's clean hook across her knees, and went down into herself to look. There was a finger and a half of water at the bottom of the well, cold and clear. It had come back on its own, slowly, since the plain. It would go on coming back whether anybody wanted to see it or not.

She did not touch it. There was nothing here to use it on.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-02-meridian/source-pool/callie/chapter-19.md

# Chapter Nineteen

There were five chairs by the bed when they came in, and Callie counted them before she had taken three steps into the ward.

Five. Set out in a half circle round the fourth bed on the left, under the window, by somebody who had been told how many there would be. Somebody had written down *all of them* at the desk yesterday, and somebody else had read it this morning and gone and got five chairs, and put them out, and gone away. Callie looked at them and did not like it, and could not have said why.

Her mother was sitting up. Somebody had helped her. She was propped against three pillows with her hair brushed and her left hand on the blanket and her right hand lying beside it, and her eyes were open and clear. She looked at the five of them coming down the ward, Katori and Pell and Ines and Otto and Callie, and her face did the thing it had done on the wagon when Callie came back from a husk line with the tin in her coat.

"There you all are," she said. "Sit down. They've counted you."

They sat down. Callie took the chair nearest the bed. Pell sat at the end with the atlas shut on his knee, and did not open it.

Across the ward, Senna was sitting up in the opposite bed with a cup of something in both hands, looking at it. She did not look over. She did not need to. The ward was one long room, and there was nowhere in it you could not hear the bed across from you.

A nurse came to pull the curtain round.

"Leave it," said Callie's mother. "She'll hear anyway. I'd rather she heard it straight."

Across the ward, Senna did not look up from her cup. But Callie saw her mouth move at the corner.

---

Doctor Sowerby came in at the ninth hour exactly, with a folder under his arm.

He was small and bald and in a hurry, just as her mother had said. He came down the ward fast and stopped at the foot of the bed and looked at all of them, one after another, the way Ines looked at a range. Then he looked at Callie's mother, and nodded, as if something had been confirmed.

"Right," he said. "I'll do this the way I always do it. The bad thing first, then the good thing, then the bill. I've been told I do it too fast. Stop me."

Nobody stopped him.

"The bad thing is that there's a growth in her head," said Doctor Sowerby. He did not look at the folder. He put two fingers against the side of his own head, on the left, above and behind the ear. "Here. Deep. It's in the part that makes words, and the part that tells the right hand what to do, and the right leg. It's been growing since before the winter. Probably a good while before." He took his hand down. "We can't take it out. There's no way to get at it that wouldn't take more of her than it takes of it. And there's nothing we have here that makes it smaller. I've looked. I looked again this morning, because I didn't want to be wrong about it."

He stopped. He looked at Callie's mother, not at any of the rest of them.

"We can't treat it," he said. "I want that said in this room with you sitting up in it. Because in a fortnight somebody in a corridor is going to say the word *treated* to your daughter, and she'll hear it as something it isn't. And I'd rather be got out of the way now."

Callie's mother looked back at him.

"Thank you," she said.

---

Callie did not hear the next part properly.

She heard it. The words went into her. But they went into her the way the cold had gone into her on the causeway, a long way down and all at once, and she was sitting in a chair by a bed in a long white room, and she was also standing on the lip of a husk line in a hayfield in the spring with her grandfather lying in the grass. And she was also on the tailgate in the dark at three in the morning, watching her mother try to braid her hair with a hand that would not take instruction. And she was also counting. She was counting something. She did not know what.

"— the good thing," Doctor Sowerby was saying.

Callie made herself hear it.

"There's swelling round it. A lot of what you've been living with is the swelling, not the growth. The headaches, mostly. Some of the hand, maybe. Some of the words." He held up one finger. "That we can bring down. There's a medicine for it. Pills, morning and night. Some people get a bit of the hand back. Some get words back. Nearly everybody gets their mornings back, without the head."

He held up a second finger.

"The fits. There's a second medicine for those. They'll come less. Maybe not at all, for a while." He put his hand down. "It will make your days better. I can promise you that, and I've got the numbers for it in this folder, and you can read them. I can't promise it makes more of them. I won't say it does."

"No," said Callie's mother. "Don't."

"And then there's the part," said Doctor Sowerby, "that everybody asks me for. So I'll — "

"Stop," said Callie's mother.

---

He stopped.

He stood at the foot of the bed with the folder under his arm and looked at her, and waited. Callie saw that he had been stopped at this exact place before, and knew what came next, and was not going to say it for her.

"I know what the next part is," said Callie's mother. "You're going to tell me how long."

"If you want it."

"I don't."

Nobody in the ward moved.

"That's your right," said Doctor Sowerby. He said it at once, without any weight on it, the way you'd say the door was on the left. "Most people want it. Some don't. It's in the folder. It'll stay in the folder. It's written down, and whoever you say can have it, whenever you say." He paused. "Including you. Next week, next month. You can change your mind about it any day you like, and I won't ask you why."

"Thank you."

"I want it," said Callie.

She had not known she was going to say it. It came out of her all at once, too loud for the ward, and across the room Senna looked up from her cup.

Her mother turned her head on the pillow and looked at her.

"I want the number," said Callie. Her voice was doing something. She made it stop. "I'm not asking to be frightened. I'm asking to know. I'm the household. The man at the window said so. For the window, it's me."

"Then the window can have it," said her mother. "You can't."

---

Callie stared at her.

"It isn't yours," said her mother. She said it very gently, and very clearly, and every word of it was in the right place. "It's mine. It's in my head. It's the one thing in there that's still mine to decide about."

"I have to know. I have to know how many — "

"I know you do. That's why." Her mother's left hand came across the blanket and found Callie's wrist. "I've watched you do a sum every morning of your life since Gaunt's Reach. Fifteen days. Nineteen days. Nine pounds of flour. Seven matches. A hundred and fifty-eight seconds. You do it before you've got your boots on. You did it on the causeway with your arms up over your face. You'd do it every morning with this, and you'd get it right, you always get them right, and you'd take one off every day before you'd said good morning to me." She held on. "I'm not giving you that one. I'm not giving you the last sum, love. You can hate me for it. I'd rather you hated me for that than did it."

Callie could not say anything at all.

"And I'm not having it myself either," said her mother, to the room now, to Doctor Sowerby. "I've spent a month on the back of a wagon counting miles. I'm not going to spend whatever I've got counting days. I'd rather spend them on something else. I haven't decided what." She let go of Callie's wrist. "That's all. You can go on to the bill."

Doctor Sowerby looked at her for a long moment.

"Right," he said. His voice had not changed at all. But he took the folder out from under his arm and put it on the end of the bed and put his hand flat on it, closed. "The bill."

---

The bill was the shortest part.

"Her care is a clinical matter," said Doctor Sowerby. "It went through at the gate, and it's owed to nobody. The pills. The bed. Me. For as long as she needs it, which is as long as she needs it, and nobody at any window in this station can tell you different. If they do, you send them up to me, and I'll explain it to them slowly." He looked round at them all. "I'm told I'm very tiresome when I explain things slowly."

"And where she sleeps," said Katori quietly.

Doctor Sowerby turned his head.

"Where she sleeps is a different window," he said. "I don't sit at it."

"I'm not sleeping in a ward," said Callie's mother.

He looked back at her.

"I've nothing against the ward," said her mother. "It's very clean. The woman opposite is the best company I've had since the spring. But I'm not living in a bed with strings up the back of my gown, being asked my name eleven times, while my daughter sits on a bench outside waiting for the afternoon." She looked at him steadily. "Can I come in for the pills? Morning and night? Like going to market?"

"You can," said Doctor Sowerby. "Most people don't want to. You'll need somebody with you who knows the fits. Who knows what to do when one comes."

"I know the fits," said Callie. It came out of her flat and small.

"She counts them," said her mother.

Doctor Sowerby looked at Callie for a moment, and then back at her mother.

"Then I'll see you in the mornings," he said. "At the eighth hour. With the girl." He picked up the folder. "You can go when you like. Today, if the nurse says. I'd rather tomorrow."

"Tomorrow," said Callie's mother. "I'll give you tomorrow."

He nodded once. Then he went away down the ward as fast as he had come, and did not look back.

---

"Well," said Senna, from the opposite bed, into the silence.

Everybody looked at her.

"There's a rule in my family," said Senna. She was not looking at any of them. She was looking at the cup in her hands, which she was holding very carefully, watching her own fingers round it. "It wasn't mine. Somebody made it in a lane, about four months ago, and it stuck. The rule is that you say the whole of it once. Properly. With everybody there. So that nobody finds out second, and nobody has to carry a piece of it round on their own." She lifted the cup and drank, looking at it, and put it down. "And then you don't let anybody say it for you again. Not in any room, to anybody. If they ask, you send them to you."

Callie's mother looked across the ward at her for a long moment.

"That's a good rule," she said.

"It's the best rule I've ever been given," said Senna. "I recommend it. That's all. I'll go back to my tea."

Callie's mother lay against her pillows. She looked at the ceiling, and then at the window over the bed, where there was grey sky and the edge of a turf roof. Then she looked at the five of them in their five counted chairs.

"All right," she said. "Once. And then nobody does it for me.

"I've got a thing in my head, and they can't get it out, and they can't make it smaller. They can make it easier. They're going to, and I'm going to let them, and I'm going to take their pills like a good woman, morning and night.

"I'm not having a number. There's a number in that little man's folder and it can stay there. Nobody in this room is to go and ask him for it behind my back, and if I find out anybody has, I'll be extremely rude, and I've been practising.

"And I'm not living in a ward. I'm going to live in a hut, with a stove, with my daughter, and with whichever of you will have me. And I'm going to walk in here every morning to be given pills, like somebody going to buy bread." She stopped and got her breath. Some of the words had come slower than others. None of them had come wrong. "That's it. That's all of it. I'd like nobody to say it for me again."

Nobody said anything.

Otto was crying. He was doing it without any sound at all, sitting very straight in his chair with his stitched arm in Callie's old shirt and the tears running down his face, and he was not wiping them.

Pell had the atlas on his knee. He did not open it. After a while he put his hand flat on the cover and left it there.

"Not everything goes in the book," he said quietly, to nobody.

"No," said Callie's mother. "Not everything."

---

Callie went out.

She did not decide to. She got up out of the chair and went down the ward and past the woman at the desk and out through the doors, and she did not stop walking until she was round the back of the long building, where there was nobody. There was a strip of wet grass between the wall and a drain, and a stack of old crates, and the grey sky.

She sat down on the crates. She put her arms round her ribs and her head down on her knees.

She did not cry. She wanted to and she could not. It was down in her somewhere too far to reach, the way the well had been on the plain when it was dry to the stone. She sat on the crates with her head on her knees and her eyes shut, and in the dark behind her eyes she was counting. She did not know what she was counting. She could not stop.

Katori found her there.

She did not say anything. She sat down on the crate next to Callie, a little apart, with her knees up and her cap pulled down, the way she had sat down on the gravel behind the pump house at dusk. She waited.

"She won't let me have it," said Callie, into her knees.

"No."

"It's the only thing I can do. It's the only thing I've got. I count. I put it on my card. It's the one thing I know how to do for her, and she won't let me."

"What would you do with it?" said Katori.

Callie lifted her head.

"The number," said Katori. "Say you had it. Say he'd told you, and it was — whatever it was. What would you do with it?"

Callie opened her mouth. She shut it.

"Count," she said.

"Yes," said Katori. "That's why."

---

They sat on the crates for a long time.

"On the hill," said Callie at last. Her voice was very small. "I gripped the hook. The first pull. I couldn't stop. I knew it was draining me and I couldn't open my fingers, because the whole wagon was on it. And it went. It went out in twenty-six. And then she said, *don't look at it. Look at me.*" She stopped. "And I didn't look at it. And it held. Ninety-one."

Katori said nothing.

"I told a boy that. Yesterday. On a bench. That you can stop holding a thing and it goes on being held. I told it to him like I knew it." Callie looked at her hands. "I don't know it. I only know it about a hook."

"Nobody knows it about anything else," said Katori. "Not the first time." She was looking at the drain. "You found it out about a hook in the middle of a hill with a wagon on it. It took you one pull. I've been trying to find it out about something else for eleven years, and I haven't got it yet." She was quiet. "Your mother's not a hook."

"I know."

"She's not asking you to stop holding her. She's asking you to stop looking at the number." Katori turned her head. "That's not the same thing. You can hold on to her with both hands for as long as there is. You just can't count it."

Callie sat with that. She sat with it for a long time, the way you sit with a thing that is too heavy to pick up yet, and you know you are going to have to, and you are not ready.

"I don't know how," she said.

"No," said Katori. "Neither did I, with the ward. You'll find out. You'll find out badly, and slowly, and you'll hate it for a long time." She stood up. "Come on. Your mother's going to want to know you haven't run off to the plain."

---

In the middle of the day Callie went to the window.

She had not planned to. She found herself in the reception yard, in front of the hatch, with Ivor Hallet behind it writing in his book. He looked up and saw her, and saw her face, and put the pen down.

"Miss," he said.

"My mother's care is clinical," said Callie. "The doctor said. It went through at the gate."

"It did."

"She's going to live in the hut. Fourteen. And come in for pills." Callie held on to the ledge. "What does it take? To stay in the hut. After fourteen days."

Hallet looked at her for a long moment through the glass. Then he told her.

He told her plainly and in order, the way he told everything. There were two gates at Meridian and they were kept apart on purpose. There was medicine, and there was residence. Medicine was not withheld from anybody, and it never had been, and the station was telling the truth when it said so. Residence was the other gate. Residence was reviewed. And a household was reviewed on what the station reckoned it contributed.

"Not on what it needs," said Callie.

"Not on what it needs," said Ivor Hallet. "I'm not going to dress that up for you."

"What do we contribute?"

Hallet looked down at his book. He turned back some pages, to where her name was.

"On paper," he said. "Three Handfuls, lifted, less station. A sponsor line in a name that came in heavy." He looked up. "And you, miss. A red thread. And a card that's gone upstairs."

"The man in the long coat."

Hallet's face did not change very much. But it changed.

"There's work in the north yard for red threads," he said. "All kinds. Most of it's ordinary and it's paid ordinary and it counts ordinary on a review. And there's one kind that counts heavier than the rest." He picked up the pen and did not write with it. "I'd rather you heard about that one from somebody who's doing it than from me."

"Who?"

"I don't give out names through this window, miss." He put the pen down again. "But you're in Yard C. You've got neighbours. And there's one of them who's signed for it, and who'll tell you the truth about it if you ask, because he doesn't seem able to tell anybody anything else."

---

Her mother already knew. Of course she did.

"Senna told me," said her mother, when Callie came back to the ward in the afternoon. "While you were at the window. Don't look like that. There was nobody else to tell me, and she wanted to, and I wanted to know." She was sitting up with a cup of tea in her left hand, and across the ward Senna was sitting up with a cup of tea in both of hers, and they had plainly been talking for some time.

"The man in eleven," said Senna. "The one who can't sit down and stand up in one evening. He's signed for four seasons on the worst work in the north yard. It's lifting ash off a stretch of road where things come through, and you have to be able to hold the road shut the whole time while people behind you sack it. Hardly anybody here can do that. So they pay it like nobody can." She looked at her tea. "He signed for my address. Not my medicine. My medicine was never the station's to sell. My address. So that I can live inside a mile of the shed."

"And you let him," said Callie's mother.

"I let him." Senna did not look up. "It's the hardest thing I've done, and I've done hard things. I came into this station on a board, and a man with a hole in his back stood up in a hut and told three children and me that he'd put his name to four years of the worst work there is, and I sat in a chair and said *I'll need somebody to push this on Tuesdays*." She drank. "Because he'd decided. And because it was his to decide. And because if I'd said no, he'd have done it anyway, and then he'd have done it without me saying yes."

Callie's mother was quiet for a long time.

"I won't let her," she said.

"No," said Senna. "You won't." She looked across at Callie at last, with those pale eyes. "Yours is thirteen. Mine was a grown man who'd already decided. That's a different sum. I'd not let a thirteen-year-old do it either, and he'd not let me let him."

---

"You don't get to spend yourself on me," said her mother.

Callie was standing at the foot of the bed. She could not sit down.

"Not seasons. Not for an address. Not for a window." Her mother's voice was very steady. "I told you at the bottom of the scarp you don't get to spend me. It goes both ways. I didn't say that part then, because I didn't know I'd need to. I'm saying it now."

"Then where do you live?" Callie heard her own voice crack and did not care. "After fourteen days. If there's nothing on the paper. If I don't — "

"I don't know," said her mother.

"You have to know."

"I don't have to know anything." Her mother put the tea down on the table by the bed, carefully, looking at it. "I've got fourteen days. I've got thirteen of them before the fourteenth. I'm not going to spend thirteen days on the fourteenth." She looked up. "When it's the fourteenth, I'll find out. Maybe there'll be something. Maybe Katori's line's bigger than the man thinks. Maybe they'll give a sick woman a hut for nothing because it's winter and nobody comes in in the autumn." Her mouth moved. "Maybe I'll sleep in the ward after all and be rude to the nurses. I don't know. And neither do you. And you're going to have to let neither of us know, for thirteen days, which you're going to find very hard, because you've never not known anything in your life."

Callie stood at the foot of the bed.

"You promised me," said her mother. "On the plain. You don't sign one thing for me without me."

"I know."

"This is me," said her mother. "Saying don't."

---

At the fourth hour a boy came down the row.

Callie saw him from the step of hut fourteen, where she had gone to sit because she could not be inside. He was about ten, in a coat too big for him, and he had a buff card in his hand the size of a hand. He went past fourteen and past twelve and stopped at the door of eleven, and knocked. When it opened he held out the card and said something, and then he stood there on the step, waiting.

Somebody inside took the card. The boy waited a moment longer. Then somebody said something to him, and he nodded and went away up the row again, the way he had come.

The door of eleven stayed open. Callie could hear voices inside, low. One of them was the dark girl's, Dessa's, flat and hard and very clear, saying something about the card. She could not hear what. She did not try to.

She did not know what the card was. She only knew that it was buff, and the size of a hand, and that the boy had waited to be told there was no reply. And that she had seen a card like that before, in a bin at a gate, with her own writing on it.

---

The man in eleven came out at dusk and sat on the step.

He did it in three movements. Callie watched him. He put one hand on the door frame and lowered himself, and stopped, and lowered himself again, and stopped, and sat. He sat with his coat over his knees and his back very straight, as if bending it were a thing he had decided not to do for a while. He was not old. He was not young either. He had a long plain face, not much hair, and the look of somebody who had been ill for some time and was being very patient about it.

He turned his head and looked at her, two doors down, sitting on her own step.

"You're the girl who reads ground," he said.

"Senna's been talking."

"Senna talks about everything. It's the only thing she's got left that nobody can take off her, and she's making the most of it." He looked at her for a moment longer. "And Toren. Toren said you read the south pasture off the grass. The whole of it. From the road."

"It's what I do."

"So I gather." He was quiet. "I'm Rook."

"Callie."

"I know," said Rook. "Everybody in Yard C knows. You and your mother came through that gate yesterday morning, and she walked, and you gave the window a card, and the window sent it upstairs." He looked at the lamp at the end of the row, which was not lit yet. "Which is about as fast as I've ever heard of anybody getting famous in this place, and I got famous here very fast myself."

---

"You signed," said Callie.

"I did."

"For four seasons."

"For four seasons. On the Sag line. It's a stretch of road at the edge of the north yard that never settled, and things come through there more than anywhere else near here." He did not look at her. "You hold it shut for a shift. The whole shift. And people come behind you and sack up what comes through, before it scatters or something else comes for it. And if you let go for three seconds, there's something between you and the people behind you." He said it flatly, like Pell reading a distance. "I haven't started. I start when I can stand up straight. Which is about a fortnight on the wrong side of when they'd like me."

"What's it like?"

"I don't know yet. I know what it pays. It pays like a joke. And the station knows it pays like a joke, and I know, and it's still the only line I had to put on a review." He turned his head and looked at her properly. "Your mother's told you not to."

"How do you know?"

"Because Senna's told me your mother's told you not to. And because I'd have told you not to, if you'd asked me first, and I'm glad I don't have to." He looked back at the lamp. "Don't sign anything because I did. That's all. I'm not a reason. I'm a man with a hole in his back who made a choice about somebody who'd already been carried three hundred miles. It was mine. It won't be yours."

"She says the same."

"She's right," said Rook.

"I know," said Callie. "Everybody keeps telling me she's right. It doesn't help."

"No," Rook agreed. "It never has. Not once in the whole history of anybody."

---

"What did you put on your card?" he said, after a while.

Callie looked at him.

"You don't have to say," said Rook. "I'm only asking because Hallet looked at me through that window a week ago with the exact face I imagine he looked at you with, and said he'd have to have somebody else look at it. And I wondered what it takes."

"*I read ground*," said Callie. "That's the first line. I told him to read them that first."

Rook made a sound. It took her a moment to understand it was a laugh, and that laughing hurt him, and that he was doing it anyway.

"That's the first honest first line that window's had in a month," he said. "Mine had a great deal in it, and none of it was that." He got his breath back. "A man came?"

"Last night. From the yard office. In a long coat. He wants me to go to the north yard when I'm not dry and show them."

"He'll have said you can say no."

"He said most people don't."

"Most people," said Rook, "haven't been told they can by somebody who isn't asking." He looked at her. "You have now. That's all. I'm not telling you to say no. I'm telling you it's allowed. It's a different thing, and people get them muddled."

Callie sat on her step with her arms round her knees.

He was turning something over in his fingers.

She had not noticed at first, because he did it the way you breathe, without looking. It was small and flat and grey, a slab of stone about the size of the palm of a small hand, rounded at the corners. He turned it over, and over, between his thumb and his first finger, the way a man turns a coin he has carried so long he has forgotten he is carrying it. He held it oddly. Not wrong exactly. As if his hand were too big for it, and had learned to go round it.

She had seen somebody do that before. Somebody sitting on a rock at the bottom of a scarp, turning a flat grey slab over and over in her fingers while Callie tried to walk in a ward.

Katori's was bigger.

Down the row, a girl with a bad limp came out of the dusk pushing a chair on wheels, with an old woman in it under a blanket, and a big boy walking beside it complaining. Rook looked up and saw them coming, and put the little grey stone away in his coat, and got himself up off the step in three movements with his hand on the door frame.

"That's the rest of my evening," he said. "Goodnight, Callie."

"Goodnight."

The lamp at the end of the row came on, flat and white and steady. Callie sat on her step and watched the chair go in at the door of eleven, and the door shut. She sat there a long time after, looking at the door, and thinking about a small grey stone that was too small for the hand that held it.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-02-meridian/source-pool/callie/chapter-20.md

# Chapter Twenty

Her mother woke on the last morning in the ward without a headache, and lay very still for a long time, as if she did not trust it.

"It's gone," she said, when Callie came down the ward at the eighth hour. She was sitting up against the pillows with her eyes very wide. "The head. The one behind the eye. I woke up and I waited for it, the way you wait for somebody who's always late. And it didn't come." She put her left hand up and touched her own temple, carefully, as if it might be somebody else's. "I haven't woken up without it since before Gaunt's Reach."

"It's the pills," said Callie.

"I know it's the pills. The little man said it would be. I'm not stupid." Her mother looked at the window. "I just didn't believe him. I thought he was being kind." She was quiet a moment. "He isn't kind. He's something better. He's right."

Her right hand still lay on the blanket like a glove. Her right leg still would not hold her. When the nurse came to help her dress, it took both of them and Callie, and her mother said a word that Callie had not known she knew. But she said it clearly, and it was the right word, and she laughed after.

Across the ward, Senna was watching over the top of her cup.

"You're going, then," said Senna.

"I'm going. I'll be back at the eighth hour tomorrow for my pills, like a woman going to buy bread."

"I'm here every morning," said Senna. "And every night, and every afternoon, and all the bits between. I've a great deal of time and nobody to be rude to properly."

"I'll come and be rude to you."

"Good," said Senna, and looked back down at her cup. "That's settled."

---

They took her home in the barrow.

It was Otto's idea. He had it the night before and would not let it go. He had gone to the south stable at dawn and got the barrow down off the wagon bed, where it had been lashed with its legs in the air since the high plain, and wheeled it the whole length of the station on its two taped bicycle wheels. Now it stood outside the doors of the long building with a folded blanket on the boards and the two lengths of cargo strapping lying across the handles.

Callie looked at it for a long time.

"I can't pull it," she said.

"No," said Otto. "I can."

He backed into it the way she had backed into it every morning of the spring, and lifted the strapping, and brought it over his shoulders. He crossed it over his chest, over her old grey shirt with his stitched arm in it, and reached round behind him for the buckle. He could not reach it one-handed. Callie did it for him. It clicked.

He took the handles, one of them in his good hand, the other one braced against his hip.

"There," he said. He went red. "How's that?"

"The left strap's twisted."

He looked down. It was. She untwisted it. He stood there in her yoke, twice the size of her, with the two lengths of webbing lying across his chest where the two dark lines were worn into the shirt, and waited.

They brought her mother out between them, the nurse and Ines, and sat her on the blanket on the boards with her back against the front of the barrow and her legs out. She put her hat on with her left hand and pulled it down.

"Well," she said, looking round from the boards at the concrete yard and the long building and the turf roofs. "This is where I came in."

---

It was four hundred yards from the long building to the second row of Yard C, and Otto pulled it at a walk. He did it carefully, with his tongue between his teeth, the way he had lifted the hound ash. The barrow creaked on its rubber tyres over the poured concrete. Callie walked beside it with her hand on the rim, not holding, only there.

She had pulled that barrow four hundred miles. She had never once seen it from the side.

It looked smaller. That was the first thing. It looked smaller and older and more mended than she had known, with the tape on the rims gone grey and the boot-sole strip on the left tyre worn nearly through. It went along at the pace of a person walking, and her mother rode on it with her hat pulled down, looking at everything.

People looked at them. There were people in the station, going about the business of a place where people lived, and they looked at a very large boy with one arm in a sling pulling a woman in a hat on a two-wheeled barrow, and a girl walking beside it with a hook. Most of them looked away again. One woman with a basket stopped and watched them all the way past.

"She's wondering what I cost," said her mother, from the boards, not quietly.

"Mum."

"I'm only saying. I'd wonder too."

---

At the corner of the row, a big boy was sitting on the step of hut eleven with a short iron bar in his left hand.

He had his right arm strapped across his chest, the same as Otto's, and his shoulder sat oddly under his coat, as if something in it was the wrong shape. He was turning the bar over in his left hand. He dropped it on the step as they came along the row. He picked it up.

He watched Otto come with the barrow. He watched the whole length of it, the strapping and the one-handed grip and the handle braced against the hip.

"You've got it on your hip," he said, when Otto was level with the step. "The other handle. You'll have a bruise the size of a plate by tonight."

Otto stopped. He looked at the big boy on the step.

"I've got one arm," he said.

"So've I," said the big boy. He held up the bar in his left hand, as if that settled it, and then dropped it again, and did not pick it up this time. "Put the strap under the handle instead of over it. It'll take the weight off your hip and put it on your chest. Your chest doesn't mind."

Otto looked down at the strap. He moved it. He took the handle again and walked two steps, and stopped.

"That's better," he said, in surprise.

"I know," said the big boy. He picked up the bar. "I've been carrying things with one arm for longer than I'd like. I've had a lot of time to think about hips."

"Thank you."

"Wyck," said the big boy, not looking up from the bar.

"Otto," said Otto, and went red, and walked on.

---

Toren was sitting on the step next to him, a little apart, with his right hand in his coat pocket.

He did not take it out. Callie saw that as the barrow went past. He was sitting on the step with his back against the door frame, looking at nothing, and his hand was in his pocket, and it stayed there. It did not come out and go back. It stayed where it was, the way a hand stays on something it has decided not to let go of and not to look at.

His face was different. She could not have said how. It was the same face as on the bench two mornings ago, tired and careful. But something in it had come down a little, the way Ines's shoulders came down half an inch when she counted six arrows.

He looked up and saw her. He nodded.

She nodded back. She did not say anything, and neither did he. She did not know what had happened, and she knew she was not going to ask, and she knew somehow that it was not bad.

---

Hut fourteen had four beds and five of them, and now six. Nobody said anything about it. Pell gave his bed up before anybody could stop him. He said he had slept on a wagon board for eleven years and a bed made him nervous, and moved his blanket to the floor by the door, next to Ines.

They put her mother in the bed by the stove. Katori took the blanket off the end of her own bed and hung it over the small deep window, the way Hallet had said to, and did not say anything about pride. The stove ticked. The room was warm.

Her mother lay in a real bed, in a hut with a stove, with a blanket over the window. She lay there looking up at the poured concrete ceiling for a long time.

"There," she said at last. "A ceiling. A real one."

"You called the sky a ceiling on the plain," said Ines.

"I did." Her mother shut her eyes. "I was wrong. This is a ceiling. The sky's the other thing. I'll remember that now."

She was asleep before anybody could answer.

---

They held the meeting round the stove in whispers, so as not to wake her.

"We're staying the winter," said Katori.

Nobody argued. Pell had the atlas open on his knee on the floor by the door. He did not write it down yet.

"The crates stay where they are till the spring," said Katori. "Nobody goes out on the high road after the frost. The man at the stable says so, and I believe him, and I'm not going to be the one who proves him wrong." She said it flatly. "Duchess mends. We find her a way to earn her keep that isn't hills. And we find five of us a winter's work."

"Six," said Callie.

Katori looked at her across the stove.

"Six," Katori agreed. "Pell."

"The ash is a winter," said Pell. "Katori's ash. For all of us. Without the stable, and without whatever they'll want for the horse, and without the review." He looked at the page. "With those, it's most of a winter. And a winter's no good to a review. A review wants to see something coming in."

"The north yard," said Otto.

"Red threads only," said Ines. "That's two of us."

"Not for everything," said Callie. "Hallet said there's all kinds of work. Most of it's ordinary." She stopped. "And there's one kind that counts heavier. He wouldn't tell me what. He said to ask a neighbour."

"The man in eleven," said Pell.

Katori said nothing. She was turning her Ember over in her fingers, the flat grey slab, the way she always did when she was thinking. She had done it on a rock at the bottom of the scarp and on a gravel lay-by and on the tailboard of a wagon a hundred times.

"Then I'll go and talk to the man in eleven," she said.

---

She did not go that afternoon. The man in eleven was not there. The door was shut and the step was empty, and Pell, who had asked at the yard office because Pell asked at everything, said he had gone to the long building to sit with the old woman.

So Katori sat on the step of fourteen instead, at dusk, with her back against the door frame and her cap pulled down, turning the slab over in her fingers. Callie sat in the doorway behind her with her knees up, because her mother was asleep inside and she wanted to be near and she could not sit still in a room.

The lamp at the end of the row came on. It put its flat white circle down on the wet concrete, the colour of bone.

The man in eleven came back along the row slowly, with one hand on the wall. He did not look at them. He got to his own step, two doors down, and put his hand on the door frame, and lowered himself onto it in three movements, and sat.

Then he took something out of his coat and began to turn it over in his fingers.

Callie watched him do it. She watched him the way she watched a ditch. She did not say anything. She had not said anything last night either, or this morning, or all day. It was not hers to say. She knew that the way she had known on the ninth day, at the tailgate before dawn, that she must not ask.

Two doors apart, on two steps, in the edge of the lamplight, a man and a woman sat turning two flat grey stones over and over in their fingers, the same way, without looking.

Katori's hand stopped.

---

Callie saw it stop. She saw Katori's head come up, very slowly, and turn, and look down the row at the man on the step of eleven.

He had the stone flat on his palm, now, the way you'd hold a thing up to the light to see it. The lamp was behind him. The stone was small, smaller than Katori's, water-grey, rounded at the corners. And on its two long edges there were two places worn smooth and a little darker than the rest, where a thumb and a first finger had gone, for years, and years, and years.

His hand was too big for it. It had learned to go round it.

Katori stood up.

She did not say anything. She put her own slab away inside her coat and buttoned the coat over it. Then she walked down the row, past the dark door of twelve, and stopped on the wet concrete a pace in front of the step of eleven, in the edge of the lamplight, with her hands at her sides.

The man looked up.

Callie did not move from the doorway. She could hear. The row was very quiet, and the stove in fourteen ticked, and somewhere down the row somebody shut a door, and then it was quieter still.

Katori was looking at the stone in his hand.

"Where did you get that," she said.

---

He did not close his hand over it. Callie saw that. He did not put it away, or move at all. He sat on his step with the stone flat on his palm and looked up at the small woman standing in front of him, a head shorter than most people, standing the way a fence post stands. He looked at her face for a long time.

Something went across his own face. It was not surprise. It was the look of somebody who has heard a voice in the next room that he had stopped expecting to hear anywhere.

"Off a floor," he said at last. His voice was very quiet. "A long time ago."

Katori said a name.

It was one word. Callie had heard it once before, in the grey before dawn at the tailgate of a wagon on the ninth day, when a woman had stood with her hands on the boards and her head down and said it to nobody. She had not caught the sound of it well enough to keep. She had known that she was never going to hear it again, and that she must not ask.

She knew it now. She knew it the way you know a bird you have only ever heard once, the moment it calls again.

"Satori," said Katori.

The man on the step shut his eyes.

"Yes," he said.

---

Nobody said anything for a long time.

The lamp stood at the end of the row. The stove ticked. Callie sat in the doorway of fourteen with her arms round her knees and did not breathe, the way she had not breathed on the first morning, watching a grey blade come up out of a closed fist.

"She had small hands," said Katori.

"She did." He opened his eyes. "I've had to hold it wrong."

Katori looked at the stone on his palm.

"May I," she said.

He did not answer. He only held his hand out a little further, flat, with the stone on it, the way you hold out something to a horse that you are not sure will take it.

Katori took it.

She took it between her thumb and her first finger. And her thumb went to one of the two worn places on the long edge, and her finger went to the other. They went there by themselves, the way a foot goes into its own print in the mud. The stone lay in her small hand as if it had been cut for it.

The man on the step watched her do it. He did not move at all. He watched the stone sit in a hand that fitted it, for the first time in eleven years, and his face did something that Callie would not have known how to describe, and would not have tried to, and would not ever tell anybody she had seen.

Katori held it. She did not look at it. She held it the way her mother held her wrist on the tailgate, for about three seconds, which was as long as either of them could stand.

---

"She was my sister," said Katori.

"I'm Rook," said the man.

That was all he said, for a while. Then: "She talked about her people. She said they'd kept words. Their own words, for things, that nobody else had." He was looking at the stone in Katori's hand. "She never said she had a sister."

"She wouldn't have," said Katori. "She was the best of us. She'd not have wasted a word on the rest." Her voice did not change at all. "She was unbearable about it."

Rook made a sound. It was not a laugh. It was the place where a laugh would have been.

"She was," he said. "She was completely unbearable."

They were quiet. Then Rook said, very carefully, as if he were putting down something heavy on a floor that might not take it:

"Her people gave the stone and the thing it's for the same name. The same word, on purpose. She told me so. And I've never in eleven years met anybody who could tell me why."

Katori looked at him for a long moment.

"I can tell you why," she said.

He waited.

"Not tonight," said Katori.

"No," said Rook. He let out a long breath. "No. Not tonight."

---

She gave it back.

She did it slowly. She held it out on her own palm, the way he had held it out to her, and he took it, and his hand closed round it the way it had learned to, too big, going round.

"You carried it," said Katori.

"Every day."

"Keep carrying it."

He looked up at her.

"I'm going to want to talk to you," said Katori. "I don't know when. Not soon. I've been holding this very hard for a long time, and I don't know how to put it down yet, and I'm not going to learn tonight on a step." She stood with her hands at her sides. "But I'm going to want to."

"I'm here four seasons," said Rook. "I signed."

Katori made a small sound. It was almost a laugh too.

"So I've heard," she said.

Then she turned and walked away. She did not walk back to fourteen. She walked past it, past Callie in the doorway, without looking at her, and round the end of the hut into the dark between the rows, where the lamp did not reach.

---

Rook sat on his step with the stone in his closed hand.

After a while he looked down the row at Callie, in the doorway of fourteen with her knees up. He did not seem surprised to see her there.

"You heard," he said.

"Yes."

"She'll want somebody," said Rook. "Not me. Not tonight. Somebody who doesn't need anything from her." He looked at the dark where Katori had gone. "Go on. I'll sit here. I'm not going anywhere at speed."

Callie got up.

---

Katori was sitting on the ground behind hut fourteen with her back against the concrete wall, in the dark, with her knees up and her cap pulled down over her eyes.

Callie sat down beside her, a little apart. She did it slowly, the way she did everything now. She put her back against the wall, and her arms round her ribs, and did not say anything.

They sat there for a long time.

"You heard me say it," said Katori at last. She did not lift her cap. "At the tailgate. On the ninth day. Before dawn."

"Yes."

"I thought you had. I saw you sitting against the wheel." Katori was quiet. "You never asked."

"You didn't want me to."

"No," said Katori. "I didn't."

Somewhere on the far side of the hut the stove ticked. Callie could hear her mother breathing through the wall, very faintly, in and out.

"She went away when she was young," said Katori. "To a place that paid for things we needed. And she didn't come back. Nobody ever came to tell us how." She said it the way Pell said a distance, as if it were a thing so flat and true it was almost boring. "Eleven years. That's all. That's the whole of it that I'm going to say tonight."

"All right."

"I've never said even that much to anybody. Not Pell. Not Ines." Katori's hand moved on her knee. "I've been carrying it very hard."

"I know," said Callie. "You said. On the hill."

"I did." Katori was quiet for a moment. "And you said you can stop holding a thing and it goes on being held."

"I said it about a hook."

"You did." Katori lifted her cap at last and looked at the dark. "He's been holding it. For eleven years. Every day. Wrong, because his hand's too big." Her voice did not change. "It fitted, Callie. It went into my hand like it was cut for it."

"I saw."

"I know you saw," said Katori. "I'm glad it was you."

---

They sat a while longer.

"I'm not leaving this station while that man's in it," said Katori. "And I can't say one more sentence to him. Not yet. Maybe not for a long time." She let out a breath. "That's a stupid place to be."

"Then stay till you can," said Callie.

"That's the winter."

"It was the winter anyway. Pell says nobody goes out on the high road after the frost. He says it like a law."

Katori laughed. It was a small tired sound in the dark, and it was the first time Callie had ever heard her do it all the way through.

"He needs lifters," said Katori, after a while. She said it slowly, the way she said things she was working out as she went. "Behind that line of his. When he can stand up straight. That's what the work is. Somebody holds the road shut, and people behind him lift what comes through before it scatters. People who know how. People who don't put grit in it." She was looking at the dark. "I've got a crew that lifts. Ines can count anything. Pell can write it down so nobody at any window can say it isn't so. Otto learned from the best there is, on the high plain, by starlight."

Callie said nothing.

"And the best lifter I've ever seen," said Katori, "is thirteen. And it's her call."

---

"My mother says I don't sign anything," said Callie.

"Lifting's not signing," said Katori. "It's wages. It's a day's work and a day's pay, and you go home at the end of it to a hut with a stove. It's what you did every day since you were eleven." She turned her head. "I'm not asking you to hold any road. I'm not asking you to put anything in that hook for anybody. That's yours, and that man in the long coat can ask for it, and you can tell him what you like."

"He says most people don't say no."

"He does."

"I haven't decided if I'm most people," said Callie.

Katori looked at her in the dark for a long moment.

"No," she said. "You haven't. That's the best thing I've heard said at this station."

Callie sat with her back against the wall and her arms round her ribs, and thought about it. She thought about a stretch of road at the edge of the north yard where things came through, and a man with a hole in his back holding it shut, and people behind him on their knees in the ash, two fingers and the flat of the hand, like cream off milk. She thought about ash lying in a seam, going nowhere, calling. She thought about her mother, asleep on the other side of this wall, who had said *don't*.

"I'll look," she said. "When he can stand up straight. I'll go with you and look at his road. And I'll read it." She stopped. "That's all. I'll read the ground. That's the first line."

"That's the first line," Katori agreed.

---

When Callie went back in, her mother was awake.

She was lying in the bed by the stove with her eyes open, looking at the ceiling, with the blanket over the window and the stove light on her face. She turned her head on the pillow when the door opened.

"Katori?" she said.

"She's all right. She's sitting outside."

"Is she." Her mother looked at her for a long moment. "Something's happened."

"Yes."

"Is it yours to tell?"

Callie thought about it.

"No," she said.

"Then don't," said her mother, and shut her eyes again. "Come and sit down. My feet are cold and there's a girl in this hut who's got warm hands and nothing to do with them."

Callie sat down on the floor by the bed with her back against the frame. She put her hand under the blanket and found her mother's foot, the right one, the one that would not take instruction, and held it. It was cold. She held it until it was not.

The others came in, one by one. Pell to the floor by the door. Ines to the bed nearest it, with her bow. Otto, who looked at Callie's hand under the blanket and went red and said nothing. Katori, last, very quietly, who took her cap off and lay down on her bed with her face to the wall and her coat still buttoned.

The stove ticked down. The room went dark except for the red line of it through the grate.

---

Her mother's breathing changed as she went to sleep. It went slower and deeper, the way it had always gone, on the barrow in the road and on the wagon bed on the plain and in the white bed in the long room with the bag on the hook. In and out.

Callie heard it.

And she began to count. She did not decide to. It began in her the way it had always begun, under everything, the way her heart began. *One.* In and out. *Two.*

She stopped.

She sat on the floor in the dark with her hand round her mother's foot, and she did not count the third one. She let it go past. And the fourth. It was the hardest thing she had ever done. It was harder than the causeway, or the span, or the hill, or taking her hand off a hook with a wagon on it and looking at her mother on the rim instead. Every part of her wanted to count. It was the only thing she knew how to do.

She did not count.

Her mother went on breathing. In and out, slow and deep, in a real bed under a real ceiling, the way she had been breathing before Callie started counting and would go on breathing without her. It did not need her to count it. It had never once needed her to count it.

Callie held on to her foot with both hands, and listened, and let it be held.

Under the edge of the blanket over the window there was a thin line of flat white light from the lamp at the end of the row. She watched it. At the first hour, the way it did every night, the way it had done every night for nine nights on the plain and seventy years before that, it went out.

Callie did not count the seconds after. She went down into herself instead, one last time, the way she always did, to see.

There were two fingers of water in the bottom of the well, cold and clear. She had not drunk anything to put them there. Nobody had. They had come back on their own, slowly, the way Katori had said they would, while she was busy with other things.

She did not use them. She did not need to. She sat in the dark with her mother's foot warm in her hands, and let the well fill.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-01-jab/chapters/chapter-11.md

# Chapter Eleven

Three hundred and forty paces, the girl had said, and Jab counted them, because his head would not do anything else.

They went along the east shoulder in a line with two lamps, the big boy called Otto pulling a two-wheeled barrow with a folded blanket on the boards and a four-year-old lying on it, and the small woman walking at the barrow's head with one hand on the rim, not holding, only there. Jab walked at the side with his brother's hand in his.

The verge was exactly where the girl had said it was, and exactly as wide, and when it ran out she said "up here" without turning round and they came up onto the made surface at a place where the ditch shallowed, and after that it was flat hard stone and the barrow stopped jolting.

"How's the head end," said Otto, over his shoulder. He was very large and he was pulling with a strap over one shoulder and the far handle braced somewhere about his hip, and he said it the way a man says a thing he has been taught to say.

"He's talking," said the small woman.

"I'm not," said Tonk.

"There you are," she said. "He's talking."

Tonk's hand was small and hot and it had a wooden fish in it as well as Jab's fingers, and it would not let go of either.

"Jab."

"Mm."

"Is it the place?"

"It's the place."

"It's got a *light* on it."

"I told you," said Jab. "Thousands of them."

"That's one," said Tonk, with the last of what he had, and Jab laughed, and somewhere ahead of them a slab of steel began to come open in the side of an earth bank with a flat white lamp burning over it in the middle of the night.

---

He said it before they had got the barrow level with the gate.

"*Clinical.*"

Four grey coats came out at a fast walk in a line, hooded, with flat clear panels in front of their faces, and behind them came a bin on two wheels with a clamp on the lid. They went past Jab entirely. They went past Otto and the barrow. They went to the crate lashed on the back of the barrow with five sacks in it, and one of them said, "Ma'am, I need you to step away from that," to the small woman, in a voice with nothing in it at all.

"Clinical," said Jab, louder. "There's a boy on this barrow and he's four years old and it's clinical."

The grey coat nearest him turned his flat panel round. Jab could see his own face in it, small and grey and streaked with something.

"Say that again."

"Clinical," said Jab. "Now, and not after the boots."

There was a pause of about two seconds in which he understood that he had said exactly the right words in exactly the right order, and that the man in front of him had been going to get there anyway and had now got there faster, and that the carter with the broken chair on his cart had bought him about a minute and had known precisely what he was buying.

"Right," said the grey coat, and turned round and shouted one word at the gate, and things began to happen very fast and entirely without reference to Jab.

---

The woman came out through the gate without a bin.

She was in a grey coat with the hood down and no panel in front of her face, about sixty, with short white hair and a lined brown face and small quick hands, and she did not run and she did not dawdle. She came straight to the barrow and looked at Tonk from the top for about four seconds without touching him, and then put two fingers on his neck and looked away over the fields while she counted.

"Hello, my love," she said. "What's your name?"

"Tonk."

"That's a very good name. I'm going to look at your leg and I'm not going to move it."

"It's *broken*," said Tonk.

"I know it is," said the woman, unwrapping. "Everybody's told me."

Nobody had told her anything. She had been out of the gate for eleven seconds.

She got the wrapping off and looked at it for a moment in the lamplight and did not make any face whatsoever, and then she said, over her shoulder, in a completely level voice, "Stretcher, and go and wake Sowerby, and tell him it's a fracture at seven weeks with a sinus and he'll want Keel."

"Wake him?"

"Wake him," said the woman.

Then she turned round and looked at Jab properly for the first time.

"And who are you?"

"I'm his brother."

"Have you been carrying him?"

"Sixteen days," said Jab.

Something went across the woman's face that he could not read at all and did not have room to think about.

"Then you'll want to hear this from me and not from a door," she said. "He's going in now. Not in the morning. Now. And you are not coming with him, and I'm going to tell you why, and then I'm going to go, because I haven't got time to be kind about it twice.

"You're carrying road dirt and you're carrying ash and you've got an open wound on that arm that hasn't been looked at, and that boy is going into a room where people are going to open him up. Everything that comes through that gate gets stripped and washed and written down, and that takes twenty minutes, and he hasn't got twenty minutes to spare and you have."

"I promised him," said Jab.

"I know," said the woman.

"No," said Jab. "You don't understand. I *promised* him. He asked me if I'd be there and I said they'd have to carry me out."

The woman put her hand on his arm, above the bandage, and left it there, and did not agree with him.

"Then you'll be there for the next one," she said. "There's going to be more than one. Say goodnight to him properly. Don't say it fast, and don't lie to him, and then let go of his hand, because if you make me take it off you he'll remember that instead."

---

He said goodnight to his brother in a road in the lamplight with two people standing by holding a stretcher and waiting for him to finish.

"They're taking you in without me."

Tonk's face did a thing. His hand went tight.

"I'll come after. It's twenty minutes and then I'll come after. That's not a maybe." He got his own hand round the small one properly, all of it. "They're going to look at your leg tonight, and there's going to be a doctor, and everybody in there is better at all of this than I am."

"You're not *better* than them?"

"I'm not better than them," said Jab. "I'm not even close, and I've been telling you I was for seven weeks."

Tonk absorbed this. Then, with a four-year-old's terrible instinct for the softest place available:

"You *lied*."

"I did," said Jab. "That one I did. I'm sorry."

"Is it the *only* one?"

"It's the only one."

His brother looked up at him from the boards of a barrow with a wooden fish in his fist.

"All right," he said. "You have to *come after*."

"I'll come after."

He let go of the hand. He made himself do it in one movement and not in stages, because stages would have been worse, and then he stood in the middle of a made road with his hands at his sides and watched two strangers lift his brother onto a stretcher and carry him in through a hole in an earth bank, and the light over the gate went out of his eyes for a moment and came back.

He did not fall down. He noticed that with a certain distant surprise.

---

Then they took everything else.

They took the five sacks and clamped them in the bin. They took Otto's barrow to be hosed and Katori's crate and the two lamps and the coats off four people's backs. They took Jab's boots, which had been on his feet since the home valley and had one short lace, and they took the pack and the hide cover and the blanket and the water skins, and they took the broken frame — he had not even seen who had carried it down the road, and somebody had, and it went into a bin in two pieces.

And then a grey coat held his hand out for the metal.

"That's on a cord round your neck," he said. "Under the shirt. I need it."

Jab stood in the road in his socks.

"It's not ash," he said.

"No, sir. It's a conduit and it's got ash all over it and it's been in something tonight." The grey coat's voice was not unkind and it was not asking. "It goes in its own bin. It gets washed and tagged and you get it back at the hatch. Nobody's keeping it."

"You don't understand what it is."

"I've took eleven hundred of them, sir," said the grey coat.

Jab put his hand up under his collar. The cord had been there for four years. It had been there through nine days of a fish run, and a summer on a jam, and a bridge, and a gravel bar with his brother's blood on his hands, and sixteen days of road, and he had not once, since he was thirteen years old, gone to sleep without the small hard warmth of it lying in the hollow of his throat.

He lifted it over his head and put it in a stranger's glove.

It weighed nothing at all. That was the thing he had never noticed. He watched it go into a small bin with a clamp on it, and watched a man tie a paper tag to the handle and write on the tag, and he stood there in the road with his collar open and one hand still up at the place where it had been, and he thought, with a kind of blank wonder: *well. Now I've got nothing at all.*

Then they hosed him down.

The water was warm. Nobody had told him it would be warm. He stood in it with his eyes shut and his face up and he did not cry, because there were six other people standing in the road doing the same thing, and because he had got out of the habit somewhere north of the creek, and because the water was warm.

---

The reception yard was a pen of poured concrete forty feet by twenty with a drain in the middle and a bench along one wall and a hatch at the far end.

There were pipes running along the wall behind the bench and the pipes were hot. Jab sat down on the bench and put his back against them and did not move for a long time.

"Oh," said Otto, two places down, in a voice of total collapse.

"Yes," said Jab.

Above them there was a light in a fitting behind a cage of wire. It was small and flat and white and it had no flame in it anywhere, and it did not move and it did not flicker, and Jab looked up at it and thought about a boy holding a hand out flat for snow at the top of a ridge and saying *thousands*.

"Don't look at it long," said the girl with the scar, without opening her eyes. "It makes the rest of the room go dark. It took me two days to work that out and nobody told me."

"How long have you been here?"

"Three weeks and a bit."

"Where were you before?"

"Four hundred miles south," said the girl, "on a wagon, with my mother on the back of it." She opened her eyes and looked at the ceiling. "Callie."

"Jab."

"I know," she said. "Your brother said it about ninety times."

---

The man behind the hatch was about forty-five with a grey cardigan under an oilskin and a pen on a string round his neck, and he had come across the whole station in the middle of the night and had a good deal to say about that to the young man beside him before he noticed that the pen was full.

"Ivor Hallet," he said, through the window. "I'm going to do this in the order it's got to be done in, and I'd ask you to let me, because out of order means starting again and it's the eleventh hour. Persons in party."

"Two," said Jab.

Hallet looked up. He looked past him at a bench with five people on it.

"There's a party of five in this yard, sir, that came in with you."

"They're not mine. They found me on the road." Jab put his hands flat on the ledge. "Two. Me and my brother. He went in through the gate about twenty minutes ago and he's four and he's called Tonk, and he's in the party."

The pen stopped for about a second and a half.

"Two," said Ivor Hallet. "One clinical, admitted at the north gate tonight." He wrote it. "That's correct, and I'd rather have it that way than the other. Name as you'd want it written."

Jab told him.

"Age."

"Seventeen."

"Household?"

"Him and me."

"Head of household?"

There was a silence in the pen that went on slightly too long.

"Me," said Jab.

"Yes," said Hallet, writing. "I'm sorry, sir. It's only a column."

---

"Declaration of capability," said Hallet, and laid a buff card on the ledge inside the window, and did not push it through.

He said the whole thing. Meridian kept a register of Kindled persons inside the line; there were about eleven hundred names on it; it was not a secret register and you could go and look at your own entry on a Tuesday. On the card went your name, your rank if you had a word for it, and a plain description of what you could actually do — not what you would like to do; what you would do at four in the morning if a wall came down. What you got for it was a band with a red thread in it, which got you through the north yard, where the work was. What it cost was that you told this window things: if you went up, if you left, if you were going to do anything in a public street that a person who was not Kindled would find remarkable.

"And if I don't?"

"Transient. Fourteen days, a grey band, no north yard, and a review, and I don't sit on the review." Hallet looked at him. "I'll save you the next question, because the whole of that bench asked it a fortnight ago and I've heard it four hundred times in nine years. Your brother's admission is clinical. It went through at the gate. It is not conditional on this card, or on your band, or on one single thing you do or don't do while you're in this station, and if anybody in here ever tells you otherwise you will come back down to this window and say so to me, and I will make it the worst fortnight of that person's life. That's not procedure. That's mine."

Jab stood at the hatch with his hands on the ledge.

"Then what is the card for," he said.

"Where you sleep," said Ivor Hallet.

---

"There's two gates at this station and we keep them apart on purpose," said Hallet. "Medicine's one. It's not withheld from anybody and it never has been and I'd put my hand in a fire on it.

"Residence is the other. Residence is reviewed. Fourteen days, and then a board looks at a household and decides what that household's worth to the station, and I want you to hear the word I used, because it isn't *needs*." He put the pen down. "I'm not going to dress it up for you at eleven at night. A household with nothing on the paper does not get to live next door to medicine that was never being kept from it. That's the shape of it. I've said it out loud at this window about nine hundred times and it has not got better with practice."

"How long will he be in there? My brother."

"That's a doctor's question and I'm not one."

"How long do people stay? With a leg like that."

Hallet looked at him for a moment through the glass.

"Months," he said.

Jab stood very still and watched the whole of the next year arrive in front of him on the other side of a pane of glass, laid out in its correct order, with a column for everything.

"Right," he said. "Give me the card."

---

He wrote it on the bench with his back against a hot pipe and a pencil that had been pushed through the hatch after the card.

It was buff and it was the size of his hand and it had lines printed on it: *NAME*, and *RANK (if known)*, and *CAPABILITY*, and under that a great deal of empty space.

He wrote his name.

Under *RANK* he sat for a while. At home they had had the words the same as anywhere else — you heard them at the racks and on the jam and in arguments about whose cousin was better at what — and his father's aunt had used one about him at fifteen and then never again, because in her opinion saying it twice made a boy tiresome.

He wrote *Flame*, and then, because it was not the whole truth, he added: *that's what they'd have said at home. I don't know what you'd say here.*

Then he sat with the pencil over the empty space for a long time.

He thought about a woman leaning past his father on a gravel bar with a baby on her back, looking at the ugliest blade anybody had ever pulled out of a river stone, and saying the only thing anybody said that night that he had kept whole.

*Well, it looks like you. That's the whole of it.*

He wrote it.

*One shape. It's a fish knife — heavy, fat in the belly, hook on the end. I've had it four years. I can hold it all day and I can light it when I need it sharp, and the light sticks at the belly every time and always has.*

*I've used it on wood, rope, hide, fish, snow, and a log jam.*

*Tonight I used it on a hound. That's the first time I've ever used it on anything that was coming at me, and I got most of it wrong, and a woman who knew what she was doing killed four while I was killing one.*
