*The light doesn't make them come. It makes them go the other way. I found that out tonight and somebody here probably knew it already.*

He read it back. Then he turned the card over, because there was no room left on the front, and wrote on the back:

*There's a second thing and I haven't got a word for it.*

*It tells me when my brother's worse. It's right about four times in five and it's wrong in the direction I want, so I don't ask it when I want an answer.*

*Twice it's done more than tell me. Once in the snow and once tonight. Both times it came on its own and both times I couldn't do it again afterward and I can't do it now.*

*It does not touch his leg. I want that written down, because somebody's going to ask me and the answer's no. It has never gone within a hand's breadth of the bone and it never will.*

He took it up to the window.

---

Ivor Hallet read the front of it. Then he turned it over, which Jab had not expected, and read the back, and then went back to the top and read the whole of it again.

He did not look up for some time.

"Sir," he said at last.

"Yes."

"This second thing. Has anybody taught you anything about it?"

"There's nobody to teach me," said Jab. "Where I'm from there's four people with a door open and all four of them have got this one." He put his hand flat on his own breastbone, over the empty place where the cord had been. "Nobody in my valley has ever had two."

Hallet laid the card down on the desk in front of him, face up, square to the edge.

"I'm going to have to have somebody else look at this," he said.

"I thought you might."

"Most people say something else at that point."

"Most people haven't just handed you a piece of paper saying they can't do the only thing on it," said Jab, and Ivor Hallet made a small sound and wrote something in his book that Jab could not see.

Then he pushed a paper band through the hatch. It went round the wrist and fastened, and there was a red thread run through the paper.

"Conditional admittance. Fourteen days, reviewable, and a household with a clinical admission comes up faster than most, because the station likes to know where it stands." He said it flatly. "Yard C, second row. There's a hut with a bed going in it and I'll write you into it tonight, and the bathhouse is shut but there's hot water at the pump end and nobody will stop you. Visiting at the long building is in the afternoons."

"No," said Jab.

Hallet looked at him.

"My brother's going into a room tonight," said Jab. "I'm not sleeping in a hut in the second row. Who do I talk to?"

"That's not how—"

"I'm not asking you to let me in," said Jab. "I know you can't. I'm asking who I talk to."

Ivor Hallet sat behind his window with a pen on a string round his neck and looked at a boy of seventeen standing in his socks with a paper band on his wrist and a hole in his forearm and his collar open where something had been taken off him twenty minutes ago.

"The woman at the desk inside the doors of the long building," he said. "Her name's Mrs Lask, and she'll tell you visiting's in the afternoons, and she'll mean it, and she has said it to four hundred people and never once enjoyed it." He picked up the pen. "And then you'll ask her the same question you just asked me. And she'll tell you it's Doctor Sowerby. And you will not get Doctor Sowerby tonight, because he's going to be busy, and you'll ask her who else, and eventually somebody will get tired of the sound of you and sit you on a bench inside the doors instead of outside them, and the bench inside is warm."

He wrote one line in his book and blotted it.

"North up the main street to the end," said Ivor Hallet. "Four hundred yards. There's three lamps on it and they go off at the first hour, and you're going to be under them when they do, so don't be frightened by it. There's a written reason."

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-01-jab/chapters/chapter-12.md

# Chapter Twelve

Mrs Lask told him that visiting was in the afternoons.

She told him at the eleventh hour of the night, standing behind a desk inside the doors of a long low building with a turf roof, and she told him without one grain of malice, the way you tell somebody which way the road goes. She was about fifty and she had a cardigan and a list.

"Who do I talk to?" said Jab.

"Doctor Sowerby."

"When?"

"Not tonight. He's in with your brother now and he'll be in with him a while."

"Then who else?"

Mrs Lask looked at him.

"There isn't an else," she said.

"Then I'll wait for him."

"Visiting," said Mrs Lask, "is in the afternoons."

"I know," said Jab. "Who do I talk to about waiting?"

It went on like that for a while. He was not rude and he did not raise his voice, and he had nothing whatever to threaten anybody with, and he had discovered at some point around the third exchange that the only currency he had left in the world was that he was still standing there.

At the twelfth hour Mrs Lask put down her pen.

"There's a bench outside the doors," she said. "I'm told it's very cold."

"All right."

"And there's one inside them, along that wall, which is for people waiting to be sent for." She wrote something on her list without looking up. "I haven't sent for you. I don't want you thinking I have."

Jab sat down on the bench inside the doors.

It was warm. There were pipes behind it. He sat there in his socks with a paper band on his wrist and his collar open, and he did not sleep, and at some point in the small hours a young woman in white came along the corridor with a tray and stopped in front of him and said, "Right, arm," in a voice that expected no discussion, and cut the field dressing off his forearm and cleaned out the hole in it and put eleven stitches in it while he watched.

"That's a bite," she said.

"Yes."

"What bit you?"

"A hound."

"When?"

"About the sixth hour."

She stopped what she was doing and looked at him properly for the first time.

"You've been sitting on this bench for six hours with a hound bite," she said.

"I've been waiting to be sent for," said Jab.

---

Doctor Sowerby came out at the eighth hour with a folder under his arm and found him on the bench.

He was small and bald and in a hurry, and he was in a hurry in a way Jab recognized instantly and trusted immediately, because it was the way his father's aunt had been in a hurry: a man moving fast because there was a great deal to do and none of it was going to be done by standing still and being kind about it.

He did not sit down.

"You're the brother."

"Yes."

"Right," said Doctor Sowerby. "I do this in the order I always do it in, which is the bad thing, then the good thing, then the bill, and I've been told I do it too fast, so stop me whenever you like and I won't mind."

"Is he alive?"

Doctor Sowerby stopped.

"Yes," he said. "I should have started there and I didn't, and that's the second time this month, and you may take it as read for the rest of this conversation that if anything of that kind changes I will say it before anything else."

He put the folder down on the bench and sat on the arm of it, which was as close to sitting down as he apparently came.

"He's alive, he's warm, he's got fluid in him and he's got the first of the medicine in him, and he's been asleep since the fourth hour, which is the best thing that's happened to him in a week. Now the bad thing, and there's a lot of it.

"The break's the least of it. I want you to hear that first because everybody gets it the wrong way round. The break is bad — it's a bad break in the shin bone, it's shifted, it's been shifted for seven weeks and it has spent those seven weeks knitting itself into a shape that is not the shape a leg is. If that was the whole of it I'd have somebody straighten it this morning and you'd have a cross child in a frame for three months and I'd be talking to somebody else by now.

"The trouble is what got in through the skin when it happened. There's an infection in the bone. Not round it. *In* it. It's been in there the better part of two months and it has killed a piece of that bone, and the piece is dead, and dead bone doesn't come back and medicine doesn't reach into it, because there's nothing alive in there for the blood to carry medicine along. It sits in the leg being a place for the infection to live, and it will go on doing that for as long as it's in him, and while it's in him he will keep having the nights you have been sitting up through, until one of them doesn't stop."

Jab sat on the bench with his hands on his knees.

"How long," he said.

"How long what?"

"If nobody does anything. How long has he got."

"I'm not going to guess at that."

"Guess," said Jab.

Doctor Sowerby looked at him for a moment.

"Weeks," he said. "Possibly not many. And I want to say the other half of that, which is that you got him here at about the last edge of the time in which any of what I'm going to say next is worth saying, and I'm told you did it on your back, and I'd rather say that to your face than have you find out in six months that everybody in this building said it to each other."

---

"The good thing," said Doctor Sowerby.

"Go on."

"We can take it out. Open the leg, take out what's dead, wash out what's left, and get the medicine to the part that's still alive. That's Keel — she does the bones and she is very much better at it than I am, and she has already looked at him once tonight, and she'll look at a picture of the inside of that leg this morning and then she'll tell you her own version of all this and it will be shorter and worse.

"While she's in there she'll take the bad union apart and set it straight, and hold it in a frame — pins through the bone, a bar on the outside, ugly as anything you've ever seen and better than plaster for a leg with a hole in it.

"And with those things, and about eight weeks of the medicine, that infection goes out of him. Not every time. Most times. I'm not going to give you a fraction because you'll do arithmetic with it."

"I'd like the fraction," said Jab.

"I thought you might," said Doctor Sowerby. "About four in five, for the infection, first time of asking. Of the one in five, most get there at the second operation. A few don't get there at all, and for those it's the leg, and I'm saying that out loud in this corridor rather than letting you find it under a door at three in the morning."

Jab sat with that.

"Four in five," he said.

"Four in five."

"And if I say do it, when?"

"This afternoon," said Doctor Sowerby.

---

"Now the bill," said Doctor Sowerby, "and I'm going to do the money part in four words, because it's four words. It doesn't cost anything.

"His admission's clinical. It went through at the gate. The operation, the frame, the medicine, the bed, the months — and there will be months — are not conditional on anything at all, and there is no window in this station at which anybody can put a price on them, and if a person tells you differently that person is wrong and you are to send them to me."

"Everybody keeps telling me that," said Jab.

"Yes, and everybody will go on telling you, because it's true and because nobody believes it until they've been told about nine times." Sowerby picked up his folder. "And now the rest of the bill, which is the part I actually meant, because money is the cheapest thing in this conversation.

"He is going to pay for this leg for the rest of his life.

"I'll give you the whole of it now and you may tell me to stop. He will be in that frame until the spring. He will not stand on it before then, and he will not walk properly on it for a good while after that, and there's going to be a stretch in the middle where he hates every one of us and you most of all, because you'll be the one who's there.

"Then there's the growing." He tapped the side of his own knee. "The place a child's leg grows from is up at the top of the shin, just under the knee, and it is a small and stupid and fragile thing, and it is very near where this has been sitting for two months. Keel will know more when she's in there and I want you to hear this as a likelihood and not a promise: that leg is not going to grow at the same rate as the other one.

"Which means that at eight he'll be a boy with one leg a little shorter than the other, and at fifteen he'll be a young man with one leg a good deal shorter, and somewhere in the middle of that there'll be a built-up boot, and probably another operation, and possibly two.

"So. He'll walk. I'd say that with confidence. He'll walk with a limp, and he'll always know the weather in it, and he will not run like other people and he will find that out at about six and it will be a bad year.

"And he'll be coming back through those doors every year until he's done growing, for somebody to measure him."

---

There was a long silence in the corridor of a long low building at the eighth hour of the morning.

"Say the last one again," said Jab.

"Every year until he's done growing."

"Here."

"Here," said Doctor Sowerby. "There's one machine in a shed at the north end of this station that takes the picture, and there's one Keel, and I don't know of another of either within four hundred miles, and I've looked, because I have a woman in that ward who has been asking me to find one for eleven years." He stood up. "That's the bill. I'm sorry to hand you it in a corridor. I'd have done it in a room with chairs if there'd been a day for it, and there isn't."

Jab sat on the bench and did the arithmetic, because his head would not do anything else, and had never once in seventeen years done anything else, and had got him and his brother four hundred miles.

Thirteen years until Tonk was done growing. Thirteen winters. Thirteen years of being inside a wall that reviewed a household every so often on what it reckoned that household was worth.

He had carried his brother out of the home valley to save his life, and he had, and the price was that neither of them was ever going home.

"Right," he said.

"I need a yes," said Doctor Sowerby. "For this afternoon. I need it from whoever's the family, and there's a column in this folder, and the woman at the desk says the window's got you down as head of household."

"I'm seventeen."

"Yes."

"There's a man four hundred miles north who'd have argued about it for a week," said Jab.

"Is he here?"

"No."

"Then it's you," said Doctor Sowerby.

Jab looked at the floor of the corridor for a moment. It was poured concrete, with a drain.

"Yes," he said.

---

They let him into the ward at the ninth hour, which was not the afternoon, and nobody explained why, and Mrs Lask did not look up from her list as he went past.

It was a long room with beds down both sides and a window over every bed and a light in the ceiling that did not flicker, and it smelled of soap and something sharp underneath.

His brother was in the fifth bed on the right, in a white gown, in a white bed, under a window.

He was asleep. Somebody had washed him — all of him, his hair too, which was lying flat and dark and clean on a pillow instead of standing up in the two directions it had stood up in for six weeks. There was a needle in the back of his hand with a tube going up to a bag on a hook. The leg was out from under the blanket in a padded trough with the wrapping off, and Jab, who had not seen it in a proper light in seven weeks, looked at it for four seconds and then looked at the window.

The wooden fish was on the pillow beside his head, where somebody had put it.

Jab sat down in the chair.

He put one hand on the bed beside his brother's hand and did not take hold of it, because the needle was in that one, and he sat there in a warm room in a dry shirt somebody had given him, and after a while his eyes did something and he put his head down on the blanket.

---

"You're the brother," said a voice from across the ward.

He got his head up.

In the bed opposite, under the other window, there was an old woman sitting up against her pillows. She was very thin and very straight, with white hair cut short and a long face that had been out in a great deal of weather, and she had a tray across her lap with a cup on it, and she was looking at him with a pair of pale eyes that did not appear to miss anything.

"They brought him in at the second hour," she said. "I was awake. I'm always awake at the second hour; it's the one thing in this building nobody's managed to do anything about. There was a great deal of running, and a boy shouting about a fish." She paused. "Nobody runs in here. I'd like that understood. I've been in this room a month and I've seen them run twice and both times it was for a child."

"He's four," said Jab.

"I know. He told the entire ward." The old woman picked up her cup. She did it slowly, watching her own hand go round it the whole way. "Senna."

"Jab."

"Yes," said Senna. "I heard that too."

---

"You've been told about medicine and residence," she said, a while later, when he had got some of the tea she had made a nurse fetch him and had discovered he could not hold the cup very well.

"Three times."

"You'll be told six more. Nobody believes it."

"I believe it," said Jab. "That's not the trouble."

"No," Senna agreed. "It never is." She put her cup down and left her hand on it, looking at the hand. "I'll tell you mine, and then I'll stop, because I'm not going to be a woman in a bed who tells a boy what to do with his life on the first morning.

"I came in on a board four weeks ago. There's a machine in a shed at the north end of this place and it is the only one of its kind anybody here has ever heard of, and I have to be within a mile of it every eleven days for the rest of my life. Eleven days. Not a fortnight if I'm careful. Eleven.

"My course is clinical. It went through at that gate and it is owed to nobody and it is conditional on nothing, and the man at the window made me a promise about it that he did not have to make and he has kept it.

"And a man I would have gone into a fire for signed four seasons of the worst work in this station so that I could have an *address* within a mile of that shed." She said it without any weight on it at all, the way you say a distance. "Not my medicine. My address. That's the whole of the trick, and it isn't a lie anywhere in it, and that's what makes it such a good one."

Jab sat with his tea.

"Did you let him?" he said.

"I did."

"Why?"

"Because he'd decided, and it was his to decide, and if I'd said no he'd have done it anyway and then he'd have done it without me saying yes." Senna looked across the ward at him. "And that's mine, and it doesn't transfer, and I want to be extremely clear that I am not telling you to do anything, because you've got a bandage on your arm and a child in that bed and you have been awake for a day and a half, and this is the exact hour at which people in this station decide things they cannot undo."

"He's going to be coming back to this building every year till he's grown," said Jab.

"Yes," said Senna. "I heard that too. This room has no walls in it worth the name and I have got very good at pretending to be asleep."

---

Tonk woke at the middle of the day, which Jab knew because he had his head on the blanket and a small hot hand landed on top of it.

"You came after," said Tonk.

Jab did not lift his head immediately.

"I came after," he said.

"You said twenty minutes."

"It was more than twenty minutes."

"It was *ages*," said Tonk, and Jab sat up and looked at his brother, who was grey and small and flat in a white bed with a tube in the back of his hand and his eyes half open, and who was, for the first time in eleven days, not making the sound he made on the out-breath.

"I know. I'm sorry."

"Where's your *thing*?"

Jab put his hand up to his open collar.

"They took it at the gate," he said. "They wash it and they put a paper label on it and you get it back at a window. That's what they do with everything here. They took my boots and they took the frame and they took you."

"They took *me*?"

"They did."

Tonk thought about that for a while.

"Did you get me back," he said.

"I'm working on it," said Jab.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-01-jab/chapters/chapter-13.md

# Chapter Thirteen

Doctor Keel came at the tenth hour and she was shorter and worse, exactly as advertised.

She was a tall woman of about forty-five with her sleeves rolled past the elbow and forearms like a person who rowed, and she came down the ward, looked at the leg for eleven seconds, put two fingers on the boy's foot, watched the toes, and then turned round and said to Jab:

"Not this afternoon. Tomorrow, ninth hour."

Jab stood up out of the chair.

"He's—"

"I know what he is. I've read it and I've looked at it and I'm going to look at a picture of the inside of it in about an hour." She said it fast and flat and without any apology in it whatsoever. "Here's the reason, and it's the only one you're getting, and it's a good one. If I open that leg today I'm opening it on a child who has had one bag of fluid and eight hours of medicine after seven weeks of being dried out on a road. If I open it tomorrow morning I'm opening it on a child who's had a day of both, and he'll be on the table for two hours either way, and the second one comes off the table better."

"And if he turns tonight?"

"Then somebody wakes me and I do it tonight," said Doctor Keel. "That's why I sleep where I sleep." She put her hands in her pockets. "Anything else?"

"How many of these have you done?"

"In a child that age, with the bone dead in the middle like that? Nine."

"How many were all right after?"

"Six were straightforwardly all right. Two took a second go and were all right after that. One I took the leg off at the knee." Doctor Keel did not look away while she said it. "He's twelve now and he's a perfectly ordinary boy and he is extremely rude about my handwriting, and I'd have him in here tomorrow to talk to you if this were the kind of station where that was allowed, and it isn't, so you'll have to take my word for him."

Jab found he had nothing to say.

"Good," said Doctor Keel. "Now go and eat something, because you are grey, and I do not operate on children whose people fall over in my corridor."

---

He went to the hatch in the reception yard at the middle of the day and got his things back, and they were clean.

The boots had been washed and dried and stuffed with paper. The pack had been hosed and the hide cover had been washed, and both of them smelled of the sharp thing they washed everything with. The water skins were there and the food bag was there with five smoked fish in it that had been through hot water and were not going to be worth eating, and the birch paper with three twists of bark folded in it, which the young man at the hatch handed over with a raised eyebrow and no comment at all.

And there was a small bin with a paper tag tied to the handle, and inside it, on a square of cloth, a thumb-length of river-worn metal on a new cord.

They had washed it. It was the first time in four years that anybody had.

He had not known it was that colour. It had been grey going green with four years of skin and smoke and river on it, and what came out of the bin was pale and clean and dull, smooth as an egg on one face and dimpled on the other, and it caught the flat white light off the ceiling in a way it had never once caught firelight. It looked like a thing off a bar. It looked exactly like the thing it had been before anybody picked it up.

"Sign there," said the young man.

Jab signed there. He put the cord over his head and dropped the metal down inside his shirt and felt it land cold in the hollow of his throat and start, slowly, to get warm off him.

He kept the tag. He did not know why. He put it in his coat pocket with his thumb over the number.

---

The picture was taken in a shed at the north end that was not a shed.

They took Tonk down on a rolling bed and Jab walked beside it, along the ward and out through a door and down a corridor that sloped, under the building, where the air got cooler and the walls got thicker and grey and there were no windows at all. At the bottom of the slope there was a door wider than the gate in the reception yard, and on the floor in front of it there was a line painted across, bright yellow, as wide as his hand.

On the wall beside it there was a sign, and Jab could read, because his mother had taught him out of the only two books in the valley.

*NO METAL PAST THIS LINE.*

"It's the machine," said the man in white pushing the bed. "It doesn't like metal. It pulls. Anything iron that goes in that room it'll have off you and across the floor at it, and it won't ask first." He looked at Tonk. "Anything on him?"

"No."

"Anything on you?"

Jab stood at the yellow line.

He had been wearing it for four years. He had handed it over once already, twelve hours ago, in a road, to a man in a glove, and that had been a thing done to him, and this was not.

"Yes," he said, and put his hand up under his collar, and lifted the cord over his head for the second time in a day, and stood there holding it.

"There's a tray," said the man in white.

"It's not iron."

"Then it's the other thing, and I've been told about the other thing, and there's a line and a tray and I'm not arguing with a boy about a machine I don't understand." The man was not unfriendly. "Tray, please."

Jab put it in the tray.

"Will they mend my leg," said Tonk, from the bed, out of nowhere, in a small clear voice.

It was the second time he had ever asked it.

"They're going to look at it first," said Jab. "With a machine. It takes a picture of the inside."

"Of the *inside*?"

"Of the inside."

"Can I see it?"

"I'll ask." Jab got hold of his brother's hand, the one without the needle. "Listen. This is a door I don't go through. It's about twenty steps and then there's a room, and the machine's loud, and you have to lie very still, and you're already very good at lying still because you've been doing it for seven weeks."

Tonk's hand went tight.

"You said they'd have to carry you out."

"I know what I said."

"You *said* —"

"I know," said Jab, and made himself keep his voice level and keep looking at him. "I'm not going to tell you they made me. I'm standing here on my own feet and I'm going to let go of your hand, because that machine would take the metal off my neck and fire it across the room, and there's a four-year-old going into that room."

Tonk looked at the yellow line on the floor.

"Is it a *rule*?"

"It's a rule."

"Do *they* keep it?"

"Everybody keeps it," said Jab. "That's the only reason it's any good."

His brother let go of his hand, which Jab had not asked him to do, and which he thought about for a long time afterward.

---

There was a bench against the wall by the line.

He sat on it with a paper tray in his lap holding a thumb-length of river metal on a cord, and the heavy door shut with a sound like a lid, and then, from behind a wall as thick as a bank, the knocking started.

It was a loud sound and a strange one. It came through in long runs, fast and steady and mechanical, like somebody hammering a pipe in another building. It stopped. It started again, faster. It went on and on, and it did not sound like a thing that could look at the inside of a person.

Jab sat on the bench and could not see his brother, and could not hear him, and could not get to him, and had, for the first time since a bridge went into a gorge in the autumn, absolutely nothing to do.

He put his hand flat on his own chest, over the place where the metal was not.

And he asked.

He did it carefully, the way he had learned, flat, not wanting anything, not reaching. He sat on a bench in a cold corridor under a building and put the question down in front of the thing under his breastbone and took his hand off it.

Nothing answered.

Nothing at all. Not up, not down, not a slope in the dark, not the smallest lean in any direction. There was the concrete and the knocking and the cold, and there was a boy sitting on a bench with an empty space behind his ribs where something used to be.

He had known it was twenty feet and a wall. He had known it was probably more than that, and that the wall had metal in it and the door was a foot thick, and he had known at the top of it all that the thing had never once come when it was called, not on a bad night and not in daylight and not for anything.

He sat with his hands open on his knees and found that he had wanted it to, very badly, and that he was going to have to be careful about that for the rest of his life.

"He's about twenty feet in," said a voice.

---

The girl with the bad leg was standing at the top of the slope.

She came down it carefully, putting her weight on one side, and sat down on the other end of the bench and stretched the bad leg out straight in front of her and looked at the heavy door, with her head a little on one side.

"On his back," she said. "He hasn't moved. There's two of them in the room with him and they're at the far side, and one of them is sitting down." She paused. "He hasn't moved at all. He's very good at it."

Jab stared at her.

"How do you know that?"

"I know where people are," said the girl, flatly, in the voice of somebody who has said it four hundred times and is tired of having to. "About thirty feet, all round, without looking, in the dark, all the time. It isn't a trick. It doesn't cost me anything. It's just there."

"All the time?"

"All the time."

"Does it ever go away?"

"No," she said. "That's the part people never ask about." She looked at him. "Dessa."

"Jab."

"I know," said Dessa. "Your brother's been saying it since the second hour."

They sat on the bench in the cold and listened to the knocking, which had got faster.

"He's still not moving," said Dessa. "Somebody's stood up."

Jab put his hands over his face.

"You can do it through the wall," he said.

"Through the wall, through the road, through the ground. It doesn't care what's in the way." Dessa was quiet for a moment. "Why?"

"Because I've got something and it doesn't reach past my own arm and it comes when it likes and it went out like a candle last night and I can't find it at all." He heard his own voice come out of him and could not stop it. "I've had it twelve days. I don't know what it is. Nobody I've ever met has one. And I have been sitting on this bench asking it whether my brother is all right through a foot of concrete like an absolute fool."

Dessa Cole looked at the door.

"I was Kindled at thirteen and nothing happened," she said. "Nothing at all, for two years. They've got a phrase for it where I come from, and the phrase is *Kindled to nothing*, and people say it in front of you." She shifted the leg. "And then one night a man told me a thing about an empty mind and an open soul, and something came on in me, and it has been on ever since and it does not go off, and I know where every person in this corridor is right now including two behind that door, and I could not tell you how, and if you asked me to make it bigger I couldn't.

"So no. I don't think you're a fool. I think you've got one that only comes when it's needed, which everybody would tell you is worse, and I'd point out that it came twice in a fortnight and both times it was needed, and I'd like to know what that adds up to." She paused. "I count things. I can't help it."

"So do I," said Jab.

"I know," said Dessa. "You counted the paces last night. Out loud. All three hundred and forty of them."

---

They brought Tonk out at the end of it with his eyes shut and his hair every which way, and he opened them as they crossed the yellow line and found Jab standing there with a paper tray in his hands.

"It *knocked*," he said.

"I heard."

"It knocked like a *man*," said Tonk, with the outrage of a person who has been personally imposed upon, "mending a roof, inside my *leg*," and Jab laughed, and put the cord back over his head, and walked beside the rolling bed all the way up the slope.

---

The afternoon was the part he had not planned for, because he had not, in sixteen days, planned for any afternoon at all.

They came in twos and threes and nobody made an occasion of it.

The big boy came first. He came in at the start of visiting with his right arm strapped across his chest inside his coat and a piece of squared steel bar about eighteen inches long held in his left hand, and he stood at the foot of the bed and looked at Tonk without any expression and said:

"You're the one that shouted."

"You're the one that didn't *move*," said Tonk.

The big boy considered this.

"No," he agreed.

"It ran at you. It ran at you and you went like *this* — " Tonk did a thing with his shoulders that cost him and that he did anyway, four inches of shoulder movement and then nothing " — and it went in the *wall*."

"That's about what happened."

"*Why* didn't you move?"

"I don't fall over," said the big boy.

There was a pause in which Jab watched his brother take this in and file it, permanently, in whatever place a four-year-old keeps the eleven or twelve facts that will turn out to have built him.

"Wyck," said the big boy, to Jab, as an afterthought, and pulled the chair round with his foot and sat down on it backward with the bar across his knees, and stayed for an hour and a half.

---

He was not good with children. That was the thing Jab watched and did not entirely understand. He did not soften his voice or ask questions in the bright way people asked children questions, and twice he said things that would have made an aunt at home put her hands on her hips.

"That's going to hurt worse tomorrow than it does now."

"*Wyck*," said Jab.

"He knows," said Wyck, without turning round. "He's had it seven weeks. He knows what it does. He doesn't know what *tomorrow's* going to do, and if I tell him it'll be fine he'll find out at the second hour in the dark that I'm a liar, and then he'll have had it and he won't have anybody."

And Tonk, in the bed, said: "Does it *stop*?"

"Yes," said Wyck.

"When?"

"Ages. And then it doesn't stop all at once, it stops a bit at a time, and one day you notice you've gone a whole morning and not thought about it, and that's the day it starts being ordinary." He held up his right hand, out of the strapping, and moved three of the fingers, and did not move the other two. "These don't work. They haven't worked since the spring. I dropped everything I picked up for two months and I was extremely unpleasant about it."

Tonk's eyes went very round.

"Can I *touch* them?"

"If you like."

Tonk pinched them, hard, with great concentration, watching Wyck's face for a reaction. Wyck looked back at him with an entirely level expression.

"Nothing," he said.

"*Nothing?*"

"Nothing at all."

"Do it *again*," said Tonk, delighted and appalled, in exactly the voice he had used about a squirrel, and Jab put his head down on the edge of the bed and laughed until his ribs hurt.

---

The small woman came at the end of the afternoon and did not sit down.

She stood at the end of the bed with her cap in her hands and looked at Tonk asleep for a while, and then she looked at the chart hanging on the rail, and then she looked at Jab, who had a paper cup of cold tea in his hands and had been holding it for about an hour.

"You've had how much sleep?"

"Some."

"That isn't a number."

"Two hours," said Jab. "On the bench."

"Since when?"

Jab worked it out and found that the answer was a long way back.

Katori put her cap on.

"Right," she said. "There's a hut in the second row with a bed going spare in it and a stove that works, and the window's got a blanket over it because of the lamp. Otto's in it. He'll wake you at the eighth hour and he'll wake you properly, because he does everything properly and it's very annoying."

"I'm not leaving him."

"No," said Katori, "you're not. That's why I said the eighth hour and not tomorrow afternoon." She stood there. "I'm going to say one thing and then I'll go. The nurse at the desk told me what you had in your coat when you came through the gate. Three twists of bark, in paper, folded in eights."

Jab did not say anything.

"Forty is what you'd count out for a road like that. Forty, at two a day. And you came in on the sixteenth day with three left, which means you've been halving them for a week, which means somebody sat down at a fire every night for a week and did a sum about his own brother's pain, and then did the sum again in the morning." She was looking out of the window over the bed. "I'm not going to say anything else about it. I only wanted you to know that somebody in this station has done the sum backwards and knows what's in it."

"Anybody would have," said Jab.

"No," said Katori. "They wouldn't."

She went to the door and stopped.

"Eat the thing Otto gives you," she said. "He'll hover. Let him."

---

The girl who read ground came at dusk and sat on the end of the bed, which nobody else had done.

"Did they tell you how long you're staying?" she said.

"Months. Years, probably. He's got to come back every year till he's grown."

"Mm," said Callie. "Mine's for good."

Jab looked at her.

"There's a thing in my mother's head they can't take out," she said, in the flat way a person says a thing they have decided to say the same way every time. "They can make her days better and they can't make more of them, and she takes pills at the eighth hour every morning in this building, and if she's not within a mile of it she doesn't get them. So we're here." She turned the tag on her own wrist over. "Four hundred miles, on a wagon, to get her to a door. And then we got to the door and everything on the other side of it turned out to be a different problem."

"Yes," said Jab.

"That's the bit nobody warns you about. The road's the hard part right up until you finish it." She looked at him sideways. "I was quite good at the road."

"So was I."

"I know," said Callie. "I read it. The frame's in a bin at the yard office and I went and looked at it this morning, and you've mended that shoulder band on the road with a strip cut off your own cover, wet, so it'd shrink on, and you took the corner off the rail and capped it instead of cutting the rail off, which is the harder answer and it's the right one." She said it the way Pell read a distance. "And your hip belt's on the fifth hole and the leather's got a memory in it at the third, so you've been getting thinner since about halfway."

Jab sat in the chair by his brother's bed and found he could not speak for a moment.

"Nobody's ever done that to me before," he said.

"It's all I do," said Callie. "People find it rude. It isn't meant to be." She stood up off the bed and pulled her coat straight. "I'll tell you the thing I wanted to tell you and then I'll go, because you're asleep sitting up.

"There's a man in eleven who signed four seasons for an address, and there's a man in a long coat who's going to come to your door with a folded paper in about a day, because he came to mine. He'll ask you nicely. He'll tell you that you can say no." She was looking at the door. "You can say no. That's all. It's true and nobody says it to you and I had to be told."

"By who?"

"By the man in eleven," said Callie. "Who signed."

---

Jab slept four hours in a hut in the second row with a stove ticking and a blanket over the window, and Otto woke him at the eighth hour by standing in the middle of the room holding a bowl of something and radiating anxiety until he opened his eyes.

He was back in the ward by the ninth.

They had taken the bag off the hook and there was a nurse doing something with a chart and Doctor Keel was standing at the foot of the bed with her sleeves already rolled, saying something to Tonk that made him look extremely suspicious.

"— and then you'll wake up somewhere else and you'll be cross, and I want you to be as cross as you like, and I want you to tell the nurse about it and not save it up for your brother."

"I *will*," said Tonk.

"Good," said Doctor Keel. "That's the whole of it, then."

"No it isn't," said Tonk.

Doctor Keel stopped.

"There's the fish," said Tonk.

---

The fish went with him. It was wood, and wood did not matter to anything, and Doctor Keel looked at it and said it could go as far as the door and would be waiting at the other end, and then looked at the boy's face and changed her mind out loud, in front of everybody, and said that it could go the whole way and she would put it in his hand herself when he was asleep.

"Sing the one," said Tonk.

"Which one?"

"The one about the boat."

So Jab sang the four verses he had left, badly, standing in a long white room at the ninth hour of the morning with about nine other people in it who were not pretending to be busy, and Tonk corrected him twice on words he was fairly sure he had right, and he let him have both.

Then they wheeled the bed away down the ward, and Jab walked beside it with his brother's hand in his until the corridor, and at the corridor there was a place where the floor changed colour, and a man in white put his hand out.

"You can come to here," he said.

"I know," said Jab.

He let go of the hand in one movement and not in stages.

"Jab," said Tonk, from the bed, going away.

"Mm."

"You have to come after."

"I'll come after," said Jab.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — books/book-01-jab/chapters/chapter-14.md

# Chapter Fourteen

He sat on the floor of the corridor with his back against the wall, because there was no bench on that side and he was not going to go and find one.

The doors were eleven feet away. They were double doors with a round window in each and a strip of rubber down the join, and every so often somebody went through them and the air moved, and Jab's whole body came up off the wall and then went back down again.

Dessa Cole arrived at about the quarter hour with a wooden chair, dragged it the last twenty feet because carrying it was not available to her, set it down against the opposite wall, and then did not sit in it. She lowered herself onto the floor beside him with the bad leg out straight, put her back against the concrete, and looked at the doors.

"You're going to want to know," she said. "So I'm going to tell you, and I'll tell you the dull bits as well as the rest, because if I only say something when something happens you'll go mad between them."

Jab did not trust his voice, so he nodded.

"Six people in there," said Dessa. "He's on the far side, about twenty-two feet, and he's the only one lying down. Four of them are standing round him and two of them are close in. One of them's moving about — that'll be somebody fetching. Nobody's fast."

"Is that good?"

"I don't know what's good," said Dessa. "I know what's fast. I've been in a room where people got fast." She shifted the leg. "Nobody's fast."

---

They were there for two hours and ten minutes, and Dessa talked for most of it, in a flat voice, about very small things.

Nobody moved for a long time. Then somebody stepped back from the table and stood still for about forty seconds and stepped in again. Then two people swapped places, which took a while, and Dessa said that this was the sort of thing people did when they were being careful about where their hands went, and Jab decided to believe her.

At the hour and a quarter, somebody went out through a door at the far side and came back with a fifth person, and Jab's stomach went over.

"Don't," said Dessa. "Everything in a place like this is somebody fetching somebody. If it were bad they'd be fetching them fast and they'd be fetching them from this side, because this side's where the rest of the building is."

"How do you know that?"

"Because my grandmother has been in this building a month and I have spent that month sitting in corridors," said Dessa, "and I count everything, and I am the only person here who knows which way the bad news walks."

At the hour and a half, Wyck came and sat down on Jab's other side without saying anything at all, and turned a piece of squared steel bar over and over in his left hand, and dropped it twice on the concrete, and picked it up.

At the hour and three quarters, Otto came with a paper cup of tea and gave it to Jab and went away again, going red.

At two hours, Katori came and stood against the far wall with her cap in her hands and did not sit down and did not speak.

At two hours and ten minutes, the doors opened.

---

Doctor Keel came out with her sleeves down and her arms scrubbed pink to the elbow and something folded over her forearm, and Jab was on his feet with no memory whatever of standing up.

"He's alive and he's asleep and he's got both legs," she said.

Then she waited about four seconds while that went in, and Jab understood, distantly, that she had said those nine words first on purpose, and that she did that every time, and that somebody had taught her to.

"Now the rest of it, and it's long, and there's a chair behind you. Sit down or don't, but don't stand there swaying at me.

"It was worse inside than it looked on the picture, which is usual. The dead piece was longer than I'd hoped — about as long as my thumb — and it was loose, which is the one piece of luck in the whole business, because loose means it came out whole and I didn't have to go chasing bits of it. I took it out, I took out a good deal of the muck round it, and I've washed that leg out with more fluid than you'd think a leg could hold.

"I've taken the bad join apart and set it straight. It's straight now. It's the straightest that leg has been since the day it broke, and it's held by four pins through the bone and a bar on the outside, and it is the ugliest object you will ever see on a child and it is going to stay on him until the spring.

"That's the good half. Here's the rest, in order.

"There's a gap in the bone where the dead piece was. That's expected and there's a way of dealing with it and it is slow and it involves him being in that frame for a long time and it involves me. I'm not going to explain the whole of it to you standing in a corridor with your hands shaking.

"The infection. I've taken out what it was living in and he's on the medicine, and in two weeks we'll know whether it's gone out of him or whether I'm going in again. I told you four in five. It's still four in five. It does not become five in five because I've had a good morning."

"And the growing," said Jab.

Doctor Keel looked at him.

"You've got a memory on you," she said. "Yes. And the growing. I could see it and I'll tell you plainly: the place that leg grows from has been in the weather for two months and it is damaged. Not gone. Damaged. So it will grow and it will not grow as fast as the other one, and I can't tell you today by how much, and anybody who does tell you is guessing.

"What that means in practice is that he will walk with a limp, and he will have one boot built up, and I will measure him every year until he stops growing, and somewhere in the middle of that I will very likely have him back on a table.

"That's it. That's the whole of the bill. He'll be asleep another two hours and then he'll wake up and he'll be sick and he'll be extremely angry, and the anger is the best sign in the world and I want you to enjoy it."

She took the folded thing off her arm. It was a cloth, and there was something in it.

"He asked me for this at the door," she said, "and it went in with him, and I put it in his hand myself like I said I would, and it's been on his chest for two hours, and I've had to wash it, and I'm told by the nurse that if I don't hand it to you personally there'll be a row."

The wooden fish was as long as Jab's thumb, smoked black by a lifetime of hanging near a fire, with one fin broken off before Tonk was born.

Jab took it, and turned round, and put his forehead against the corridor wall, and stood there with his back to all of them for about a minute, and nobody in that corridor said one word to him or to each other until he had finished.

---

Tonk woke at the middle of the afternoon and was sick, and was extremely angry.

He was angry about the sick. He was angry about the bar on his leg, which he could see, and which he described in detail to everybody who came near the bed. He was angry about the tube in his hand. He was angry that Jab had not been there when he woke, which Jab had, and which he had been asleep for, and which did not matter in the slightest.

"You *weren't*."

"I was."

"You *weren't*, and it was ages, and nobody *told* me —"

"All right," said Jab. "I wasn't."

Tonk lay back on the pillow, mollified and exhausted.

"It's got a *bar*," he said.

"I saw."

"It's got a bar and it's got *nails in my leg*."

"Four of them."

"*Four?*"

"Four," said Jab. "I counted them."

His brother lay and looked at the ceiling of a long white room and thought about it for a while, and Jab sat in the chair and watched the whole machinery of it turning behind a four-year-old's face.

"It's the rail," Tonk said at last.

"What is?"

"The bar." Tonk moved one hand, vaguely, at the leg in its cradle. "It's the rail. Like on the frame. So it doesn't *hang*."

Jab looked at the thing bolted through his brother's shin and did not say anything for a moment, because his throat had shut.

"Yes," he said. "That's exactly what it is."

"They did it *inside*," said Tonk, with enormous scorn, and went to sleep.

---

The cord was in the pack, because they had washed the pack and everything in it and given it all back with a tag on.

Jab took it out that evening and tied one end round his own left wrist with a knot that would not draw, and looked for somewhere to put the other, and there was a rail on the side of the bed, painted white.

He tied it to the rail and then untied it, and put the end into his brother's hand instead, and closed the fingers over it, and Tonk's fist shut on it in his sleep without waking up at all.

"He won't need that in a week," said the young woman in white, going past with a tray. "There's a bell on a string."

"I know," said Jab.

She stopped.

"I'm not telling you to take it off," she said.

"I know," said Jab.

---

The man in the long coat came on the second morning.

He was tall and thin and about fifty, with a close-cut grey beard and a quiet face, and he came to the door of the hut in the second row where Jab was not living so much as keeping a bed, and asked for him by name, which he had got off a card.

"I'm from the yard office," he said. He did not give a name. "I've read your declaration."

"All of it?"

"Both sides." He had a folded paper in one hand and he did not offer it. "I'll tell you what I came to say and then I'll say the other things. What I came to say is that there's a Flask of ash on the board in your name."

Jab stood in the doorway.

"The hound at the culvert head," said the man. "It was lifted the same night by the crew that was working the line, and it's been weighed, and there was a disagreement at the yard office about whose name it went against, which I am told lasted some time and was settled by a small woman in a cap who does not raise her voice." He almost smiled. "It's yours. Less station, as everything is. It's not a great deal of money. It's more than nothing and it'll be on the paper at your review, and I'd rather you heard about it from a person than found it on a wall."

"I didn't kill it for money."

"No," said the man. "Nobody's ever killed one for money in the whole history of this station. They kill it and then there's money, and that's a different sentence and it took me about nine years to learn to say it the right way round." He put his hands behind his back. "Now the rest.

"Your household's reviewed in twelve days. That's you and a child in a bed in the long building, and on the paper at the moment there's a Flask of ash and a red thread and nothing else, and I'm not going to pretend to you that that's a strong sheet.

"Your brother's care doesn't depend on it. You've been told that, probably four times, and it is true, and I'll say it a fifth if you like. What depends on it is whether you go on sleeping inside this berm, within four hundred yards of him, or whether you're a transient, outside, coming in on a pass on visiting days."

"And what puts something on the sheet?" said Jab.

"Work," said the man in the long coat. "And there's all kinds of it, and most of it is ordinary and pays ordinary and counts ordinary." He paused for exactly as long as it takes a person to change the subject without appearing to. "And there's the north yard."

---

He explained it standing on the step, without any dressing on it at all, which Jab came to understand afterward was itself a kind of technique.

There was a stretch of road at the edge of the north yard that had never settled. Things came through there. The station kept a line on it, and a man to hold that line shut, and a crew behind him to lift what was left of whatever came through, because what was left was reserve-ash and reserve-ash was fuel and money and the reason anybody went near the place at all.

Holding the line shut was a narrow trade. Almost nobody at Meridian could do it, and the ones who could were worth whatever the station had to pay them, which was why the station did not pay them in money.

"You can't hold a road," said the man. "Your card says so and I believe it.

"What you can do is the other job, which is that when the line goes down — and it goes down; it went down four times last winter and twice this autumn — the people sacking the ash are on their knees with their backs to whatever's just arrived, and somebody has to be standing over them with something sharp until the line comes back up. At the moment there isn't a somebody. There's a man with a hole in his back who holds the road and then has to try and do both.

"It is the worst work in this station. It pays like a joke, and everyone involved knows that it pays like a joke, and the reason it pays like a joke is that the people who take it have got nothing else to put on a review." He held up the folded paper, briefly, and put it back in his coat without giving it to anybody. "Four seasons. It's a thing you sign.

"And it would count. I'll be honest with you about that, because if I'm vague you'll find out in twelve days and you'll think I was clever. It would count heavier than anything else you could put on that sheet, and it would very likely settle the question of where you sleep for the next four years, which by my arithmetic is about a third of the time your brother has to keep coming through those doors."

Jab stood in the doorway of a hut in the second row with a paper band on his wrist.

"You've done that arithmetic," he said.

"I do it every time," said the man. "I'd be no use here if I didn't." He looked at Jab levelly. "And I'll tell you the thing I'm supposed to tell you, which is that this is a request, and that you can say no."

"Do people say no?"

"Most people don't," said the man in the long coat.

---

"There's one more thing," he said, at the end of the row, having turned round.

"The second thing on your card. The one you haven't got a word for."

Jab said nothing.

"There's a column for it," said the man. "It's a wide column and there's very little in it. Nobody's going to come and take you anywhere, and nobody's going to ask you to do it in a yard for an audience, and if anybody does you may tell them I said they were not to." He paused. "But you wrote it down, and you didn't have to, and I'd like you to know that a person read it. That's all. We'd like to understand what's on the register. That is genuinely the whole of the sentence and there isn't a second half to it."

"It doesn't come when I call it," said Jab.

"No," said the man. "It says that on the card. In three separate places, actually, which I noticed."

Then he went away down the row, past the door of hut eleven, where a man was sitting on the step with his coat over his knees, turning something small and flat over and over in his fingers, and did not look up.

---

Rook was still there an hour later.

Jab had not planned to go and stand in front of him. He had walked the length of the row twice and then a third time, and on the third time the man on the step said, without raising his voice:

"You've been up and down four times. Sit down or go in, but don't wear the path out, it's the only one I've got."

Jab sat down on the step of hut twelve, which was empty, two doors along.

They did not say anything for a while. Down at the end of the row the lamp on the pole was not lit yet.

"He came to you as well, then," said Rook.

"This morning."

"Long coat. Grey beard. Doesn't give a name and it isn't a trick, he just genuinely doesn't think it's relevant." Rook turned the flat grey stone over in his fingers. "He's not lying to you. I want to start there, because a lot of people would rather he was. Every single thing he told you is true and he told it to you in the right order and he didn't leave the worst of it out."

"He said most people don't say no."

"He says that to everybody," said Rook. "He said it to me on this step in the rain about five weeks ago. And then I signed, four days later, standing up, in a room, and I have not once decided it was the wrong thing and I have not once felt clean about it either."

"Why did you?"

"Because there's a woman in that long building who has to be within a mile of a machine in a shed every eleven days for the rest of her life," said Rook. "And her medicine was never the station's to sell, and they never tried to sell it, and they didn't have to, because they own the ground the shed stands on and a bed is a different window." He was looking at the unlit lamp. "It's an extremely good arrangement. It's the best one I've ever seen. Nobody in it is even doing anything wrong."

"That's the part I can't get round," said Jab.

"No," Rook agreed. "Nor me. I've been at it five weeks."

---

"How old are you?" Rook said, after a while.

"Seventeen."

"And the boy's four."

"Four."

"And there's nobody else."

"There's nobody else," said Jab.

Rook nodded slowly, and did not say any of the eleven things that people had been saying to Jab since the gate.

"Right," he said. "Then I'm going to tell you one thing and it's the only thing I've got that's worth anything, and somebody had to say it to me twice before it went in.

"You can say no.

"That's all. I'm not telling you to. I'm not telling you not to, either, and if you came to me in a week and said you'd signed I'd not say one word about it, because I'd have no standing to and because I'd understand exactly why." He put the stone away in his coat. "I'm telling you it's allowed. People get that muddled with being told what to do, and they're different things, and nobody ever says the first one out loud in this place because everybody's busy being reasonable at each other."

"What happens if I say no?"

"Then you're reviewed in twelve days on a Flask of ash and a red thread," said Rook, "and I don't sit on the review, and neither does he, and I'm not going to lie to you about which way it goes."

"Then it isn't a choice."

"No," said Rook. "It's a choice with a price on it. That's what a choice is. The other kind's a menu." He got his breath. "And I'll say the last of it and then I'm going in, because I'm not supposed to sit out here in the cold and there's a girl of fifteen who counts how long I do it.

"Don't decide tonight. There's twelve days. Everything in this station is designed so that you decide it at three in the morning on the second night, when you've just come off a road and you're frightened and you'd sign anything to stop the feeling. That's not anybody's plan. It's just what the shape of the place does to people, and the only defence against it is to know it's happening."

---

Tonk asked about the squirrel on the fourth day.

He asked it out of nowhere, in the afternoon, lying flat with the bar on his leg and the ceiling to look at, in the middle of a conversation about something else entirely.

"When we go back," he said, "I'm putting the thing on the heap."

Jab, who had been folding a blanket, went on folding it.

"You said," Tonk went on, with the absolute confidence of a man quoting a signed agreement. "You said when my leg's mended I walk up there with a pocket full of something, and I do it *myself*, and you'd show me the tree. You *said*."

"I did say that."

"So when's my leg mended?"

Jab put the blanket down and sat on the edge of the chair and looked at his brother, and made himself do the thing he had made a rule about six weeks ago and had kept, every single time, even when it cost him a night.

"Your leg's going to mend," he said. "It's going to take until the spring before you stand on it, and a while after that before you walk properly, and it's never going to be quite the same as the other one. That's all true and you'd have got it out of somebody in this building by the end of the week anyway.

"And we're not going back."

Tonk's face did not do anything. That was what it did.

"Not ever?"

"Not to live. It's sixteen days of road and you've got to come to this building every year until you're grown, and I'm not going to tell you a story about it. That's what it is."

"But the *heap*."

"I know."

"You *said*." His voice went up and broke. "You said I could *put it back* — "

"I know what I said," said Jab, "and I meant it when I said it, and I can't do it, and I'm not going to pretend I can." He got his brother's hand, the one without the needle in it. "So here's what I've got instead, and you can tell me it's not as good, because it isn't.

"That squirrel got robbed by me. Not by you. You never took anything off anybody in your life. So it was always going to be me who owed it, and what I owe him I can't pay, because he's four hundred miles north and he's had his winter and he's either got through it or he hasn't."

"Then it doesn't *count*," said Tonk, furiously.

"It counts," said Jab. "That's the whole trouble with it. It counts and I can't pay it where I took it." He looked out of the window over the bed, at a turf roof and a grey sky. "So I'll have to put it back here."

"Here?"

"Here," said Jab. "Somewhere in this place. To somebody. I don't know who yet and I don't know what with." He heard himself say it and heard what it meant about four minutes after his mouth had finished. "That's what it is when you can't get back to the heap. You don't get out of it. You just have to find a different heap."

Tonk lay and thought about this for a long time, with his brows down, doing the arithmetic of a four-year-old, which was not the same arithmetic but was not nothing either.

"All right," he said at last. "But I'm doing it. Not you."

"You're doing it."

"*Me.*"

"You," said Jab.

---

The lamp at the end of the row went out at the first hour that night, all at once, the way it did.

Jab was on the step of the hut in the second row with his back against the door frame, because the ward turned the visitors out at the tenth hour and he had discovered that he could not lie down yet. His brother was four hundred yards away in a white bed with a bar bolted through his leg and a wooden fish on the pillow and a twelve-foot cord in his fist, and somebody would come and get him if the cord was pulled, because he had stood at a desk and asked who he talked to until they wrote it down.

Three days ago he had been on a road with everything he owned on his back.

He went through it now, the way he had gone through the ledger every night for sixteen days, because his head was not going to let him do anything else.

He had a bed in a hut that was his for twelve more days. He had a paper band with a red thread in it. He had a Flask of ash on a board with his name beside it, less station. He had a piece of river metal at his throat, washed clean by strangers for the first time in four years, and a shape on his fist that four people in a valley four hundred miles north would have recognized across a river. He had a second thing that had come twice, both times without being asked, and had not come since, and would not come now if he sat here all night and begged it.

He had a brother who would keep his leg, probably, and who would limp, certainly, and who would be coming back through those doors every year for thirteen years.

And he had a folded paper that a man had shown him and not handed over, which would answer the whole of it, for four seasons, on the worst work there was.

He had not said yes. He had not said no. He had gone four days without saying either, which was the longest he had put off a decision since the accident, and he was not sure yet whether that was cowardice or the only sensible thing he had done since he came through the gate.

The road had taken sixteen days and he had been good at it.

He sat on a concrete step in the dark in a place with a written reason for everything, and thought: *I don't know how to do this one at all.*

---

"Jab."

It was the next afternoon, and the light through the window over the bed had that thin winter quality that made everybody in the ward look like they were underwater, and his brother had been quiet for a while.

"Mm."

"You know when we were on the hill."

"Which hill?"

"The *last* one." Tonk's hand moved on the blanket. "With the smoke. And you showed me and I said what's it called and you said Meridian and I said no, *it*."

Jab put the cup down.

"I remember," he said.

"And you said I had to see it up close first."

"I did."

"I've seen it," said Tonk.

He had. He had seen a gate come open in an earth bank at night with a lamp burning over it. He had seen a room with a light in the ceiling that did not move. He had been inside a machine that knocked like a man mending a roof. He had had a piece of his own bone taken out of him by a woman with her sleeves rolled up, and a rail bolted on the outside of his leg so it did not hang, and a wooden fish put into his hand by somebody who had not had to.

"Well, then," said Jab. "Has it got a name?"

Tonk looked at the ceiling for a long moment.

"Not yet," he said.

---

# Book 2, Movement 1 — Ash Collection Scene (drafted)

Status: `POSSIBLE` — drafted, not yet placed into a full movement draft. Written
per owner request for a more technical, in-depth treatment of ash collection
with audible self-talk from Rook. Positioned for Movement 1 ("The Liability
Clause"), after the clan's first real call-out since Meridian's wall closed
around them — texture/characterization, does not advance or resolve any
Movement 1 plot thread (Toren's failed Guard attempt, Dessa's/Wyck's arcs,
Cinder Company, the disk mystery all stay untouched by this scene).

Canon-checked against the locked manuscript (Ch. 4, 7, 16, 21, 24, 25) before
finalizing — no flask/drinking-container introduced (that mechanism belongs to
the Hobb's Wall edition only), no POV shift into Rook's interiority (Book 1
was consistently Toren's POV throughout; this stays exterior, all technical

---

He came down off the roadbed alone with the sack of jars across his shoulder, and above and behind him the noise of the others went on being ordinary — Wyck saying something twice, and Dessa not answering it, and then the flat knock of a tailboard going up.

The two of them had come apart about forty feet from each other, which was the whole of the difficulty.

"Flask," he said, of the drift up on the cart track, and did not break stride. "Flask and a fair one. Handful in the ditch and it's a dirty Handful, and you'll be lucky." He came down onto the stone. "Four minutes. Same four minutes for the both of you, and I've got one pair of hands, so one of you is going into the ground."

He set the sack on the dry stone and took two jars out of it and worked their bottoms into the grit until they stood on their own, and drew both stoppers and laid them in the mouth of the sack where they would not roll.

Then he went to the good one first, because that is the order.

He put his hand flat on the track in the middle of it, the whole palm, heel down first and the fingers laid after it the way a man sets a plank, and pressed until the ash packed up under him and there was nothing at all between his skin and it.

"Flat," he said. "All of it flat. Fingers too. There."

Under his hand the grey dulled and went on dulling, out from the heel of it in a circle that widened and slowed and stopped, and he lifted the palm and moved it a span and set it down again and the circle started over.

"Don't haul," he said. "It isn't rope. It comes at the rate it comes, and pulling at it doesn't get you more of it, it gets you less — no. No. There. There, that's it."

He worked along the drift that way, on one knee, a span at a time, and what he lifted he held, and he held it low and open at his side with the fingers apart, and did not close them.

"High," he said. "Hold it high. It'll go down through you if you let it, and then you've got the ache tomorrow and the road's got the money." And after a moment, further along, in the same flat voice: "Nothing in it to want anything. That's the whole of why you can carry it at all. Nobody home, and you're the better hole."

Behind him the ash in the ditch went on going into the ditch.

He did not look at it.

When the track was done he took his palm off the stone, and where he had been there was a patch of it soft-edged all the way round like a puddle drying, and what was still lying in it had gone colorless, and the wind that had not been able to shift any of it an hour ago came up the cut and took that.

He came up with the hand low and took the first jar and tipped its mouth against his palm and held it there, and it went down out of him into the glass and was grey again by the time it lay in the bottom.

He put a finger into it and took it out and looked at what was on the finger, and rubbed it off between finger and thumb, slowly, and wiped the hand on his coat.

"Hand before lamp," he said. "Old saying and a true one, and nobody standing at a scale has ever once believed it." He turned the jar a quarter and did not hold it up to anything. "Clean. That's clean, and that's a fair Flask, and I'll say so in front of whoever's weighing."

He worked the stopper in with the heel of his palm, and stopped with his hand still on it.

"Thinner, though."

He said it to the jar.

"That's thinner than it ought to come off a thing that size, and it was thinner at the weighbridge, and I said nothing then either." He set it down against the sack. "Or I'm old and my hand's going. I'd sooner it was me."

The ditch had had most of the rest. What was left lay along the black shallow in a smear with the light still crawling through it, and the smear was short now, and going.

He went down the bank into the water anyway and put his knee in it and his hand on the bottom.

It dragged.

"There it is," he said. "That's silt. That's four feet of ditch bottom coming up with it, and it'll weigh dirty, and whoever's on the scale will write it dirty and be right to." He moved the hand two inches over and it dragged again. "Come on. Come *on*."

He had his head turned toward the bank when the last of it went, because something had moved up there or had not, and he was a moment late with it, and what he had not got yet went out from under his palm and down into the mud.

"No," he said.

He looked at the place where it had gone in.

"Thirty years, and you turned your head."

He came up the bank and put what he had into the second jar, and it was not much, and he stoppered it and set it beside the first. Then he sat down on the dry stone with his forearms across his knees and his hands hanging off the ends of them.

They were not steady. He did not put them away. There was nobody down there to put them away from.

Up on the road Wyck laughed at something, once, and stopped.

After a while Rook got up and packed the jars into the sack with a fold of canvas between them, one and then the other.

"Flask and a fair one," he said, to nobody at all. "And a dirty Handful, less what the ditch got."

He went up.

---

## OWNER VOICE — APPROVED

# Monroe Jackson — Owner Voice

Status: owner-confirmed seed. Revise only from accepted owner edits. This layer
governs aesthetic disagreements with the general Monroe Light profile; canon and
movement authority still govern story facts.

## Shared prose instincts

- Prefer readable, controlled sentences for the main narrative line.
- Split a sentence when it asks the reader to carry several turns at once—action,
  explanation, retrospective judgment, and metaphor—unless accumulation is the
  intended emotional effect.
- Long sentences are available, but they must create purposeful momentum or
  association. Length by itself is not sophistication.
- Choose the word that precisely matches the judgment and the viewpoint's natural
  register. For example, the owner preferred “chosen poorly” to “chosen badly” in
  measured narration. This is contextual evidence, not a ban on “badly.”
- Preserve direct owner wording. Automated repair may work around an owner-authored
  passage but may not silently restyle it.

## Fantasy

No fantasy-only owner preference has enough accepted evidence yet.

## Science fiction

No science-fiction-only owner preference has enough accepted evidence yet.

## Learning discipline

- One edit is an example, not a universal law.
- Promote a broad instinct only when several accepted edits support the same
  reader-facing preference, or when the owner states the preference directly.
- Do not learn voice from continuity fixes, factual corrections, typographical
  repairs, rejected suggestions, reverted edits, or untouched AI prose.
- Do not carry names, setting facts, or plot events from an example into a new
  movement. Learn the difference between WAS and NOW, not the example's subject.
